Abstract: Point cloud segmentation is a fundamental task in 3D data analysis, with applications across robotics, augmented and virtual reality, and remote sensing. However, the inherent sparsity, noise, and unordered nature of point clouds introduce significant challenges, particularly in achieving reliable classification. While the state-of-the-art rely on training models for explicit modeling of uncertainty, there is a gap in exploring different roles that estimated uncertainty using generative approaches can play. This work investigates the impact of deterministic uncertainty in point cloud segmentation by explicitly modeling epistemic uncertainty and using it in different roles, namely, as a feature and in loss function. We evaluate a baseline artificial neural network (ANN) and two uncertainty-aware variants (uncertainty as an additional input feature and as a per-sample confidence weight in the training loss) on four large-scale LiDAR benchmarks spanning airborne and mobile acquisition, namely ISPRS Vaihingen, AHN3, Paris-Lille-3D, and Toronto-3D, together with PointNet++ and RandLA-Net references and their uncertainty-augmented counterparts under canonical spatially disjoint train and test splits. Under this protocol, uncertainty-aware modeling improves mean Intersection over Union, recovers minority classes that the baseline ANN never predicts at all, and in half of the configurations improves the alignment between prediction error and estimated uncertainty as measured by Patch Accuracy versus Patch Uncertainty. Our findings indicate that uncertainty can play multiple roles in the segmentation pipeline rather than as a device for improving accuracy alone.

</details>


### [109] [ChangeJEPA: Predict Before You Compare for Remote Sensing Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05570)

Abstract: Remote sensing change detection is typically formulated as Siamese feature comparison, where two images are encoded independently and a decoder determines which discrepancies correspond to meaningful change. This is challenging because the encoders preserve both true scene changes and nuisance variations such as illumination, seasonal, radiometric, and geometric differences. We introduce ChangeJEPA, a bitemporal joint-embedding predictive architecture that reformulates change detection as latent post-state prediction. Given a pre-change image and partial post-change evidence, ChangeJEPA predicts the full post-change representation under two complementary conditions: a prior hypothesis derived from the pre-change scene and an updated hypothesis conditioned on observed post-change context. A joint-embedding predictive loss aligns both hypotheses with the target latent representation, encouraging semantic consistency while suppressing transient appearance variations. Change is then inferred from the disagreement among the pre-change representation, the observed post-change target, and the predicted latent states, capturing complementary evidence of change, prior surprise, contextual update, and prediction reliability. Across SYSU-CD, LEVIR-CD+, and CDD, ChangeJEPA consistently outperforms its decoder matched Siamese baseline by up to 1.51 IoU points and achieves the best IoU and F1-score on all three benchmarks, demonstrating the advantage of latent post-state prediction over direct feature comparison. Page: https://buddhi19.github.io/ChangeJEPA

</details>


### [110] [Whose Values Are Generated? Measuring Visual Value Leakage in Multilingual Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05578)

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) systems serve users across languages and cultural contexts. However, evaluations of cultural representation focus mainly on recognizable elements such as landmarks, clothing, and activities, overlooking the cultural values that generated images convey. To bridge this gap, we introduce VisionValueBench, a multilingual benchmark that operationalizes six of Hofstede's cultural value dimensions (e.g., individualism versus collectivism) to investigate how T2I systems frame neutral social situations through a particular cultural orientation. We find that value-neutral prompts, which admit diverse cultural interpretations, nonetheless yield images that favor particular value orientations, a phenomenon we call visual value leakage. We evaluate prompts varying in language and geographic cues across 960 social scenarios and six T2I configurations. Generated images consistently favor the same value orientations across all six dimensions, for example, equality over hierarchy (low power distance), individual autonomy over group obligation (individualism), and enjoyment over restraint (indulgence). Prompt language has limited influence on these orientations, whereas geographic cues shift some dimensions toward the associated cultural values while largely preserving the prevailing orientations. These findings reveal substantial homogeneity in the cultural values conveyed by T2I systems worldwide.

</details>

We present a modular framework in which slide-level embeddings, obtained by training an attention-based aggregator over patch embeddings from a frozen foundation model, and per-visit clinical embeddings are fed into 15 interchangeable fusion methods with a shared output dimension and prediction head (except decision-level methods). Using a prospective cohort of newly diagnosed inflammatory bowel disease patients, we predicted STRIDE-II-aligned remission outcomes from baseline or follow-up visits. The best fusion method reached macro-F1 of 0.57-0.75, outperforming both single-modality models in four of six scenarios, and differed by outcome and input. Image reliance, the drop in balanced accuracy when the slide embedding is removed, tended to follow the remission type - highest for histological and lowest for endoscopic remission; methods with similar accuracy relied on the slide to very different degrees.

Abstract: Can a neural network learn to render 3D Gaussian splats on its own, with no rasterizer in the renderer? We present GaussianRenderFormer, a transformer that takes a set of Gaussians and a camera and outputs the image directly. Each Gaussian becomes one token in a view-independent encoder, and a view-dependent decoder turns small patches of camera rays into pixels by attending to those tokens. Trained on 26,820 Objaverse objects pruned to 20,000 Gaussians each, GaussianRenderFormer renders 300 unseen objects at 38.08 dB PSNR against the full, unpruned splats, while rasterizing the same pruned input reaches 44.02 dB. The gap is not one of generalization, and our diagnostics are consistent with a bottleneck in the view decoder: telling it the projected footprint of each Gaussian, which it could in principle infer, adds 1.4 dB. Under-training is not ruled out. Code, trained models and videos: https://svlwoof.github.io/gaussianrenderformer

</details>


### [113] [CAPTOR: text-encoder-free grounding by counting caption co-occurrence over the visual words of a frozen encoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05599)

Abstract: Grounding a text query in an image needs a matrix scoring every term against every patch. Vision-language models compute it with an aligned text encoder, which many image encoders, such as histopathology-related models trained on slides alone, lack. The Caption-Anchored Patch-Term Occurrence Representation (CAPTOR) estimates the matrix without a text encoder. It clusters a frozen image encoder's patch tokens into visual words and describes each captioned image by its share of patches in each word. A term signature is the standardized difference of those shares between images whose captions mention the term and the rest. The matrix factorizes into a term-by-word table and a query-independent image factor, so an image is encoded once and a new term adds one table row. Against every training-free baseline, CAPTOR leads on Common Objects in Context (COCO), Pascal Visual Object Classes (VOC) and Cityscapes. On COCO, scored on the cells one object owns, it reaches 0.565 mean intersection over union (mIoU) against 0.502. On all three, it is also level with a text alignment trained on the same backbone, which needs gradients, a text encoder and several times the captions. On held-out breast-cancer slides, it exceeds the strongest baseline scoring patches with a pathology text encoder by 0.160 mIoU. Read through CAPTOR, UNI, an encoder trained on slides alone, exceeds such baselines by 0.132 on a common grid. Counting can therefore give a text interface to any image encoder trained without language, wherever captioned images exist.

</details>

Abstract: Many temporal approaches for video recognition rely on explicit cross-channel aggregation projections, which have quadratic complexity in the number of channels, introducing significant computational overhead. In addition, the spatio-temporal representation used for video recognition is biased either toward local order interactions (for CNNs) or toward global order interactions (for transformers). Starting from the multi-order spatial representation of MogaNet, our method introduces two complementary contributions to tackle these problems. First, for the temporal modelling, we introduce parallel multi-order processing with depthwise-only convolutions and implicit gating-based aggregation, which is linear in the number of channels, inducing a considerable computational reduction. Second, we propose a dual interaction between spatial and temporal dimensions, which allows a better spatio-temporal representation without increasing the computational cost. By combining these two contributions, our proposed VideoMogaNet achieves performance on par with or better than state-of-the-art action recognition approaches across multiple video benchmarks (UCF101, HMDB51, Kinetics-400, Diving48), while considerably reducing the cost of the temporal branch.

</details>

Abstract: Modern railway perception requires large comprehensive image datasets, but creating them is slowed by manual labeling, especially for track polylines and other complex infrastructure geometry. This paper presents the Active Railway Infrastructure Dataset Annotation framework, a railway-specific semi-automated pipeline that converts instance predictions from a mask scoring instance segmentation network into bounding-boxes, polygons, polylines, and paired polylines. To keep automatic track geometry annotation reliable without ground truth, it applies confidence thresholds and an unique contour-agreement quality score as an acceptance gate. An active learning loop ranks remaining images by uncertainty and adapts the number of manual queries per cycle. This is achieved through a novel quantity control mechanism, in which easy images are auto-accepted and human effort targets hard cases. Experiments on disjoint, diverse imagery and time-sequential, redundant imagery quantify effort reduction and accuracy trade-offs, achieving about two thirds reduction in the disjoint regime and up to 93% in the sequential regime. The study also provides guidance for initial label budgets and stopping to avoid over-annotation.

</details>

Abstract: Adversarial diffusion distillation has conventionally relied on image features for discrimination. We revisit this choice for multimodal diffusion transformers (MMDiTs), whose text stream becomes image-dependent through joint attention. We introduce Text-Stream Adversarial Distillation (TSAD), which exploits intermediate text-token states as a new adversarial representation. Because the text stream has already read the image while remaining tied to the text condition, changing that condition changes what information it extracts from the image. Building on this property, we introduce Aspect-Cued Discrimination (ACD), which conditions the discriminator on different aspects of the sample, such as typography, objects, or style, to steer which properties are emphasized during adversarial training. Building on the expert-discriminator design of NitroFusion, we integrate TSAD and ACD in NitroFusion2. NitroFusion2 brings few-step distillation from the conventional 4-8-step regime down to just 1-2 steps at comparable quality. At one step, it produces sharp rendered text and rich fine-grained details, making one-step generation practical rather than merely a stress test for distillation. At two steps, it remains competitive across the evaluated benchmarks. These results establish the MMDiT text stream as a powerful and steerable representation for adversarial diffusion distillation.

</details>

Abstract: Background: The automatic identification of tree species from bark photographs is of practical interest for forestry inventory and the wood-processing industry, yet the design choices that govern the accuracy of deep-learning approaches have not been studied systematically, and some prior results may be inflated by leakage of images of the same trunk across training and test partitions. Methods: A systematic ablation study is conducted on the public BarkNet 1.0 dataset (20 species) under the established tree-level, leakage-safe partitioning scheme. Using ConvNeXt-V2 backbones, the following factors are varied in turn and evaluated by five-fold cross-validation: patch size, the strategy by which per-patch predictions are aggregated into an image-level decision, the training regime (single- versus two-stage), backbone capacity, the whole-image baseline, and the number of patches available at inference. Results: Per-patch accuracy rises monotonically with patch size (from 75.6% at 160 px to 89.6% at 512 px), and image-level accuracy rises in step, plateauing near 94.9% around 512 px. Learned aggregation exceeds majority voting by at most 0.7 pp, an advantage that shrinks as patches grow and attention becomes near-uniform. Backbone capacity (pico to tiny) alters accuracy by less than a point, and two-stage training offers no advantage over one-stage. Whole-image classification (91.9%) trails patch-based aggregation, since it admits no bag to average over; accuracy increases with the number of patches at inference, saturating around 64. Conclusions: The study yields a simple recommended configuration: patches as large as possible while leaving enough to average over (around 512 px here), with a nano backbone. It also quantifies the second-order role of learned aggregation and model capacity, providing a reproducible reference point for practitioners and future research

</details>

Abstract: Coarse-to-fine stereo matching enables efficient disparity estimation but remains sensitive to errors in propagated disparity, which may place ground-truth correspondences outside subsequent local search ranges. We propose TRUST-Stereo (Teacher-Assisted Robust Uncertainty-Guided Search and Transfer for Fast Stereo Matching), which addresses this limitation through an Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM). UGRSM estimates the reliability of propagated disparity and predicts residual offsets to adaptively relocate local cost-volume centers. Additionally, we introduce a two-stage Senior-Junior Teacher Assistant knowledge distillation strategy to transfer rich monocular representations from a large foundation model to a lightweight backbone to achieve fast stereo matching performance. Experiments on KITTI 2012, KITTI 2015, ETH3D, and Middlebury demonstrate strong zero-shot generalization. Using only 1.4M synthetic stereo pairs, TRUST-Stereo achieves state-of-the-art results on several benchmark metrics, outperforming competing efficient methods trained with substantially larger datasets, including real-world pseudo-labeled samples. Specifically, it achieves a KITTI 2015 EPE of 0.93, ETH3D Bad 1.0 of 2.13% and Middlebury Bad 2.0 of 6.79% while maintaining inference at approximately 50 ms per stereo pair.

</details>


### [119] [Anchored Slice Propagation with Boundary Transport for 3D CT Segmentation from Three Orthogonal Annotated Slices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05693)

Abstract: Sli2Vol propagates a binary mask through affinities between adjacent slices, but an error can persist because each prediction becomes the input to the next step. Three orthogonal annotations reduce dependence on any one chain, yet they offer more than three starting masks: where a chain crosses another annotated plane, the correct label is already known. Those labels could correct drift during propagation and indicate how much to trust each chain at fusion. Between annotated planes, the information carried by the chain may matter as well. A binary mask records foreground membership but discards distance to the boundary, a cue that could help follow gradual shape changes between slices.We address these two sources of error with a fixed pretrained affinity backbone. We anchor each chain by correcting its prediction where it crosses another annotated plane, then use its agreement with those labels to weight fusion. We compare this anchored mask with two ways of transporting boundary geometry: a truncated signed-distance field (SDF) propagated by dense affinity averaging, and a contour whose vertices follow motion cues from the same affinity. The contour tracker starts from a hand-crafted rule and learns a motion correction from supervoxel pseudo-objects in 506 CT volumes, without manual organ labels. On 2,091 held-out structure instances from approximately 403 CT scans spanning 17 abdominal structures, anchoring raises mean Dice from 0.737 with orthogonal mask propagation to 0.769 and improves every structure. Beyond the anchored mask, learned contour tracking improves Dice by 5.7\% for the esophagus and 5.9\% for the postcava, while SDF transport improves right-adrenal Dice by 2.8\%. The anchored methods have similar mean Dice (0.769--0.772); contour tracking performs worse on the kidneys, stomach, and hepatic vessel tree.

</details>

Abstract: Finding specific objects, structures, and spatial patterns supports critical work in agriculture, infrastructure planning, environmental monitoring, and disaster response. These features, however, are often dispersed across very large regions and span dramatically different spatial scales, which defines a search space that is costly to embed, maintain, and search naively. We introduce GeoDIVE, a model that treats a multiresolution satellite image gallery as a forest and uses best-first traversal to determine which tiles to encode. At each visited node, a lightweight model then predicts an optimistic estimate of the best query similarity hidden among its descendants using the query and subtree representations. In evaluations with image and open-vocabulary text queries, GeoDIVE matches exhaustive search at 10% of the encoding cost for image queries and leads all tested baselines at every budget in both modalities. To support future evaluation of multiscale geospatial search, we also release object-level image and text queries for Git-10M, constructed from Microsoft building footprints and OpenStreetMap polygons with VLM-generated captions. Source code and models will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Owing to the prohibitive cost of manual annotation for enormous medical images, self-supervised learning (SSL) has gained substantial attention and shown promise in various medical imaging tasks. Among SSL approaches, contrastive learning has emerged as a prominent one, encouraging models to encode semantic information that remains invariant between different augmented views. However, this invariance can be spurious for medical images, as subtle but critical medical features can be suppressed during augmentations. In this paper, we mitigate this issue by encoding the learned feature embeddings as probability distributions rather than fixed vectors, thus capturing a richer and more nuanced spectrum of information. Furthermore, we propose a unified probabilistic framework that seamlessly integrates multiple SSL approaches. Our method achieves superior performance over the state-of-the-art SSL methods on various downstream tasks across multiple datasets, including medical image segmentation and classification. We further validate the method through additional analyses of robustness to stronger augmentations, label efficiency under limited fine-tuning labels, and computational trade-offs. The code is released at https://github.com/wwzjer/ProbSSL.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal brain MRI provides complementary anatomical, tissue, and pathological information to support downstream artificial intelligence (AI) applications. However, modality availability is uneven across public and pooled neuroimaging datasets: T1-weighted (T1w) MRI is widely represented, T2-weighted (T2w) and fluid-attenuated inversion recovery (FLAIR) MRI are less consistently available, and gadolinium-enhanced T1-weighted (T1Gd) MRI is relatively scarce. Pooled datasets therefore contain participants with different combinations of acquired MRI contrasts, reflecting variation in the study designs, acquisition protocols, and clinical or research objectives of the contributing studies. Many missing-modality benchmarks simulate incomplete MRI by withholding contrasts from complete four-modality cases, which may not represent the observed availability patterns in pooled datasets. To address this gap, we introduce C-WFDM, a ControlNet extension of our Wavelet-Fusion Diffusion Model (WFDM), for subject-specific missing-modality completion. C-WFDM is built on an earlier BrainScape snapshot comprising 157 public MRI datasets and 45,880 preprocessed scans. The pretrained WFDM backbone retains target-modality and metadata conditioning, while target-specific ControlNet adapters use MRI contrasts from the same participant to guide synthesis. C-WFDM synthesizes T2w from T1w, FLAIR from T1w and T2w, and T1Gd from T1w, T2w, and FLAIR. Acquired conditioning contrasts are used when available, and generated intermediate contrasts are used only when required. We compare C-WFDM with ALDM, cWDM, and Pix2Pix using paired structural similarity index (SSIM), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), and mean absolute error (MAE). In the BrainScape completion experiment, C-WFDM achieves the best T2w and FLAIR results among the evaluated models, with SSIM values of 0.9232 and 0.8904, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) are bottlenecked by the length of their input token sequence. Standard patchification fixes this length with a single patch size applied across the image, regardless of regional content. Prior work on adaptive patch sizing reduces token count by assigning coarser patches to visually simple regions, but relies on noisy pixel-level heuristics such as local entropy to make patchification decisions. We argue that this compute allocation should be grounded in the model's interpretation of task relevant information, not in raw pixel statistics. We introduce Split Policy Learned Image Tokenization which learns to allocate token resolution based on what the model finds meaningful, producing shorter token sequences that are better matched to task-relevant content. SPLIT matches the accuracy of full-resolution ViT classification baselines while using 30% fewer tokens. The same gains hold on dense prediction: SPLIT achieves competitive accuracy on COCO and ADE20K, demonstrating that semantically aligned patchification generalises well beyond classification.

</details>


### [124] [Decompose the Distillation: Interpretable Single-Pass Guidance for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05755)
*Wuyang Zhang,Xiaolin Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Classifier-Free Guidance is a widely used inference-time guidance rule for conditional image generation with diffusion models, which exposes a guidance scale that adjusts conditioning strength after training. This flexibility requires conditional and unconditional model evaluations at every sampling step, so an N-step trajectory takes 2N forward passes. Single-pass alternatives reduce such cost, yet most collapse CFG's two components into one opaque guided prediction, losing the separable base flow and guidance direction that support per-step control and diagnostics, even when the scale remains adjustable. This work introduces CtrlFlow, a single-pass guidance method that preserves CFG's separable base-flow and guidance-direction interface. To preserve both components at one-pass cost, CtrlFlow attaches two lightweight LoRA adapters to a frozen backbone, each adding 2.5% parameters, and predicts the base flow and guidance direction from one pass of the shared frozen backbone. Because the guidance scale multiplies the guidance direction only after the forward pass, one evaluation yields the guided velocity at any scale, so a single trained checkpoint remains adjustable at inference without retraining. Preserving two outputs introduces a training mismatch: inference amplifies guidance-direction errors by the chosen scale, while naive component-wise losses weight both outputs equally. To align training with this composed inference-time error, CtrlFlow uses a unified multi-scale distillation loss. Residual guidance errors can still persist at extreme scales, where even small guidance-direction errors are amplified by the guidance scale. To reduce this inference-time error, CtrlFlow applies scale-adaptive modulation to damp velocity-field errors and time-grid warping to concentrate solver evaluations where the trajectory changes most rapidly.

</details>


### [125] [ME^2FDiff: Multiscale Expert Enhancing Feature Diffusion in Residual Network for Brain Tumor Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05766)

Abstract: Automated segmentation of post-treatment glioma from MRI is essential for accurate diagnosis and treatment planning, yet remains difficult. Surgery and chemoradiotherapy cause structural changes in brain like resection cavities, edema, and fibrosis, that alter imaging appearance and mimic tumor recurrence. Contemporary segmentation architectures attain high accuracy with two limitations. First, the bottleneck originates from the deepest encoder stage, so one representation must serve sub-regions that differ widely in size and appearance. Second, they produce the segmentation in a single forward pass with no mechanism for refinement. We propose ME^2FDiff, a single-model architecture, addressing both. An attention-conditioned spectral diffusion block refines the bottleneck. It uses the image-conditioned latent diffusion process with two processes: a spatial pre-attention gate and a spectral fusion block whose timestep-adaptive bandpass filter selects the informative frequency band at each noise level. A multiscale expert-enhanced feature block fuses the intermediate encoder scales into the bottleneck through expert-selected shared tokens, guaranteeing balanced utilization without an auxiliary loss. On the BraTS 2024 post-treatment glioma dataset, ME^2FDiff achieves the best results on every tumor sub-region among all single-model baselines (91.23% Dice, 84.52% IOU, 2.13 mm HD95 on whole-tumor) and surpasses a three-expert ensemble on both overlap metrics at roughly one-third of its inference cost. Against recent state-of-the-art methods retrained under identical conditions, it maintains the strongest performance with the fewest parameters of any competing model. These findings demonstrate that a single, compact network can absorb the specialization, usually sought through ensembling or larger architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Holistic perception for autonomous agents requires reliably segmenting and associating scene elements across time and camera views, including objects outside a fixed semantic taxonomy. Existing open-set panoptic benchmarks focus on individual images or views, while panoramic tracking benchmarks assume closed-set taxonomies. Consequently, these benchmarks do not evaluate whether unknown objects can be consistently segmented and tracked across synchronized multi-camera sequences. We introduce WayPOP, a panoramic open-set panoptic tracking benchmark built on real Waymo PVPS sequences augmented with diverse out-of-taxonomy 3D assets from Objaverse. Inserted objects are placed at fixed locations in the 3D scene and rendered using calibrated vehicle-mounted cameras as the ego vehicle and surrounding traffic move, providing temporally and cross-view consistent panoptic annotations and auxiliary LiDAR range observations. WayPOP contains approximately 670 curated assets instantiated as 980 unique unknown-object tracks, yielding 53,452 unknown-object mask annotations across 21,300 camera images. Our evaluation protocol defines a hierarchy of tasks from view-independent open-set panoptic segmentation to full panoramic open-set panoptic tracking, using known/unknown PQ and (w)STQ metrics. We benchmark representative open-set and open-vocabulary segmentation methods with temporal and cross-camera association. Our results reveal substantial limitations of current methods in truly open-set scenarios, highlighting robust unknown-object localization and identity association across time and views as major open challenges.

</details>

Abstract: Recent LLM-centric advances can generate attractive furnished rooms by selecting and arranging assets. Yet evaluation remains limited to specific perspectives or predefined questions, failing to reflect overall scene quality. To address this pressing need, we introduce GREP. Its core is a focus shift from defining specific questions to establishing a three-tier evaluation philosophy: basic physical validity checks whether a scene obeys physical constraints; visual coherence concerns compatibility in object scale, style, and composition; and, at the highest level, human-use logic requires object arrangements to align with everyday human habits. Guided by these principles, an evaluation agent autonomously and adaptively identifies issues from scene context, gathers evidence, and produces scores and reports. This supports scene-specific issue discovery and reliable judgments. Experiments show that (1) GREP achieves 87% agreement with human majority preferences, substantially outperforming baseline evaluators. (2) GREP reports improve scene quality through refinement, whereas score-only and no-feedback baselines show slight declines. (3) GREP suggests that as planning ability improves, recent models such as GPT-5.6 Sol can move from relying on pipelines and harnesses to challenging their guidance and constraints, but still lack the ability to resolve the resulting conflicts. Stronger models are therefore expected not only to plan well, but also to adapt to external guidance and constraints more flexibly. Project page: https://han-808.github.io/GREP/

</details>


### [128] [ReTailor: A Residual Feature Tailoring Framework for Real-Time Cross-Sensor Pansharpening Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05811)

Abstract: Recent pansharpening models achieve strong fusion performance on trained sensors, yet often generalize poorly to unseen sensors. Existing cross-sensor adaptation is limited by target-sensor retraining, which requires target-sensor training pairs and a dedicated training stage, and by per-image zero-shot adaptation, which is computationally expensive due to whole-network optimization for each test image. To address these problems, we propose ReTailor, a real-time sequential cross-sensor adaptation framework. Only a lightweight residual Feature Tailor is rapidly optimized under self-supervised, physics-aware constraints to correct cross-sensor shifts without modifying the well-learned fusion prior. For efficient and effective deployment, ReTailor combines content-aware patch selection, which identifies a few informative patches per image for optimization at negligible cost to improve both quality and efficiency, with rolling residual bootstrap, which inherits sensor-related knowledge across the image stream to further improve quality. Extensive experiments on cross-sensor adaptation settings show that ReTailor mitigates cross-sensor degradation both effectively and efficiently. Moreover, we compare ReTailor with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), a widely used method for fine-tuning, to demonstrate the advantages of our approach. We further evaluate the core principle of ReTailor on two additional remote sensing tasks: super-resolution pansharpening and image segmentation, where it consistently improves performance. In general, ReTailor provides a highly effective and efficient strategy for deploying pansharpening models under real-world cross-sensor degradation, while also offering useful insights into managing cross-sensor degradation in other remote sensing tasks. Code will be released at https://github.com/JingZe-Liang/ReTailor .

</details>


### [129] [Navigational Safety of Marine Surface Vehicles in Restricted Visibility: From Intelligent Vision to Vision-Centered Multi-Sensor Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05818)

Abstract: Marine surface vehicles (MSVs) have attracted considerable attention for their commercial, scientific, and military applications. Owing to their favorable performance-to-cost ratio, shipboard cameras are widely used to detect and recognize potentially hazardous situations across diverse operational scenarios. However, visual perception is inevitably degraded by restricted conditions, including irregular motion of the imaging platform, adverse weather, and long imaging distances. To improve situational awareness and navigation capabilities, complementary sensors, such as the automatic identification system (AIS), radar, and light detection and ranging (LiDAR), etc., have become essential shipboard equipment. Multi-sensor fusion can provide richer perceptual information and help ensure navigational safety of MSVs in restricted visibility. This article reviews recent progress from intelligent vision to vision-centered multi-sensor fusion for newcomers and researchers in intelligent ship navigation and intelligent waterborne transportation system (IWTS). After introducing the generation of quality-degraded images in restricted visibility, we will review intelligent vision methods for four key issues, i.e., shipboard video stabilization, visual perception enhancement, object detection and tracking, and navigable area segmentation. We then examine vision-centered multi-sensor fusion methods, including infrared-visible image fusion, radar-vision fusion, LiDAR-vision fusion, AIS-vision fusion, and other multi-sensor fusion scenarios. Open-source datasets and safety-critical applications are also summarized and discussed. Finally, we highlight open challenges and future research directions. The collected maritime datasets are available at https://github.com/MAPGroup/Datasets and will be regularly updated.

</details>

Abstract: Digital human e-commerce video generation requires preserving product appearance, human identity, and overall video style during dynamic demonstrations. Although recent models support multiple reference images, their general conditioning schemes do not explicitly distinguish the asymmetric roles of "what is presented" and "who presents it." Consequently, generated videos may favor a single reference, resulting in product distortion, identity drift, or single-reference collapse. To address this issue, we propose DualCast, a role-addressed dual-image conditioning framework for video generation. By explicitly modeling the distinct roles of product and human conditions, DualCast reduces role ambiguity and feature competition between references. First, a dual-spatiotemporal slot injection mechanism VAE-encodes the product and human images separately and places their latent features at the front and rear boundaries of the DiT sequence, enabling sequence-level condition separation and directed injection. Second, a mask-guided region-weighted loss strengthens supervision over product, face, and product-face overlap regions, improving generation fidelity in critical areas. Third, image condition dropout enables a single model to support dual-image, product-only, and human-only generation. In addition, we construct a curated dataset of 10K e-commerce videos for model training. Experimental results demonstrate the strong performance of DualCast.

</details>

Abstract: Various land-use models characterize land-use patterns and facilitate scenario planning. However, existing models are largely predictive, forecasting transitions based on numeric data without synthesizing new land-use maps from simple text prompts. Meanwhile, generative AI in urban systems remains unimodal, weakly controllable, and evaluated mostly by generic image quality rather than domain-specific metrics. To address these gaps, we introduce DualControlNet, a generative land-use model that jointly synthesizes high-resolution land-use maps and satellite imagery, addressing multimodality, controllability, and verification challenges. Trained on over 40,000 paired tiles from Orlando and Chicago, DualControlNet is evaluated using land-use, spatial consistency, and image quality metrics. Relative to unimodal baselines, DualControlNet improves land-use diversity consistency by 62.8% and building coverage ratio consistency by 16.3%, more than doubles cross-modal spatial consistency, and triples visual quality. Scenario-based experiments demonstrate that DualControlNet supports sustainable development through multidimensional controls, increasing density and diversity, and responding to text prompts like "high-density development." Overall, this work advances land-use modeling via a multimodal, controllable, and verifiable generative framework, lowering technical barriers and enabling intuitive exploration of urban futures.

</details>

Abstract: Identity-preserving video generation aims to synthesize high-quality and visually realistic videos from textual and visual conditions while faithfully maintaining the subject's identity across diverse motions. Prior methods have extensively introduced reference-guided control signals and identity-aware conditioning to improve facial alignment from reference images. However, improving static identity alignment alone creates a fundamental tension between identity fidelity and temporal controllability, which frequently causes identity drift or motion suppression under large pose variations and multi-segment sequential actions. To address this, we propose Chronos, a framework that unifies single-segment facial identity preservation and multi-segment sequential action generation under a common temporal formulation. Specifically, Chronos integrates Semantic Prompt Anchoring to adaptively refine the user prompt, Visual Frame Anchoring to transform conditions into visually grounded keyframe anchors for visual guidance, and Metric-Guided Candidate Routing to select the highest-quality videos from heterogeneous generators via multi-dimensional evaluation. Extensive evaluations demonstrate the superiority of our approach, which achieved first place in both Track 1 and Track 2 of the ACM MM 2026 Identity-Preserving Video Generation Challenge, demonstrating its effectiveness across both facial identity preservation and sequential action generation settings.

</details>

Abstract: Saliency maps, offered as evidence that mammography classifiers use the cancer, are untested in dense breasts, which can hide cancers. We tested whether they locate cancers worse there, and why. On 1,345 malignant CBIS-DDSM views, we scored three CNNs' Grad-CAM, Integrated Gradients and occlusion maps by whether their peak hits the lesion and a chance-normalized lesion-to-background saliency ratio (bits; +1 doubles it). Unadjusted gaps were large: Grad-CAM peaks hit 44% of dense vs. 58% of non-dense lesions. Case mix, chiefly more calcifications in dense breasts, explained about half or more of each gap. Occlusion kept a significant average of -0.56 bit gap. Controls also implicate the classifiers: unadjusted gaps exceeded an image-only conspicuity map's, and models relied less on dense-breast lesions. We recommend reporting density gaps before and after case-mix adjustment, per classifier, with conspicuity and lesion-reliance controls.

</details>

Abstract: Humans can easily recognize the same objects across different viewpoints of a 3D scene, whereas teaching models such multi-view-consistent perception typically requires videos or calibrated multi-view captures, which are considerably more expensive and less diverse than large-scale monocular data.~In this paper, we introduce Feature Puzzles, a novel framework for learning multi-view-consistent segmentation using only single-image semantic segmentation supervision. Specifically, we propose a simple yet effective pipeline that constructs multi-view-consistent segmentation pseudo-labels through image cropping and geometric transformations. We further introduce a lightweight, plug-and-play network that leverages the multi-view-consistent image features learned by existing pretrained 3D foundation models and the generated pseudo-labels to perform multi-view-consistent segmentation. Importantly, our flexible framework supports various types of prompts provided from one or multiple views and can be readily extended to open-vocabulary 3D segmentation. Under this single-image supervision, our feed-forward model achieves the best mIoU on NVOS, remains competitive on SPIn-NeRF and ScanNet++ against baselines using per-scene optimization or in-domain multi-view training, and demonstrates zero-shot transfer to open-vocabulary 3D segmentation. Visual comparisons are available on our project page: https://featurepuzzles.github.io/

### [135] [DecoupAttn: Decoupled Sparse Attention for Training-Free Acceleration of Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05917)

Abstract: Sparse attention accelerates few-step video diffusion transformers by computing a few key blocks exactly and compensating for the rest. However, existing sparse attention still faces key challenges, including identification overhead from finding critical blocks at every denoising step, suboptimal budget allocation that sets sparsity budgets uniformly or by local metrics, and cross-step error accumulation as reused masks or clusters go stale. Observing that the errors left on skipped blocks cancel each other once every key is compensated, we propose DecoupAttn, a new paradigm of decoupled sparse attention: semantic clusters compensate every key, and exact attention corrects their error on a few blocks fixed once offline by position. To support this paradigm, we introduce Distortion Budget, a rate-distortion objective for budget allocation that places exact computation across timesteps, layers, and heads where it removes the most distortion that compensation leaves in the final latent. Built on this budget, we further propose Reassign Cache, in which each step reassigns the drifting keys to cached centroids and refreshes compensation while the budgeted blocks stay fixed. Moreover, DecoupAttn is training-free: a few calibration clips compile one plan offline, and inference runs standard block-sparse kernels with one fused compensation kernel. Our code will be released after submission.

</details>


### [136] [FLINT: Coupling Proximal Initialization and Bounded Stochasticity for Flow-Matching Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05945)
*Junseo Bang,Daewon Choi,Dong Ju Mun,Se Young Chun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 解析错误

Abstract: Flow matching models have emerged as a dominant paradigm for generative posterior sampling in imaging inverse problems. Existing solvers follow two distinct lines of work: trajectory-space methods, which refine sampling dynamics from random initial noise, and source-space methods, which optimize the initial noise itself. While most prior work treats these as independent design choices, we show that they are inherently coupled. We derive a unified stability bound that links initialization proximity and cumulative sampling stochasticity through a multiplicative envelope, complemented by two empirical observations. First, we identify a sufficiency regime in which a moderately optimized proximal source initialization is enough to attain the performance plateau, rendering exhaustive source-space optimization unnecessary. Second, this initial advantage is fragile: it is eroded by excessive stochasticity accumulation during guided sampling, necessitating a mechanism that bounds the cumulative noise injection. These findings motivate FLINT, a two-stage framework that operationalizes this coupling. FLINT establishes a high-fidelity starting point via a manifold-constrained proximal objective in only a few iterations, then performs guided sampling under a bounded stochasticity schedule that helps prevent erasure of the initialization advantage. Across diverse inverse problems, FLINT delivers state of the art perceptual quality and competitive distortion metrics while maintaining superior computational efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) enables multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to scale capacity efficiently, but expert parameters dominate memory even though only a few experts are activated per token: they account for over 90% of model size in modern MoE-MLLMs (e.g., 54 GB out of about 58 GB in BF16 for InternVL3.5-30B-A3B and Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B). Expert pruning has emerged as an effective compression strategy, but existing methods aggregate importance uniformly across tokens, ignoring that the same expert may serve different roles for vision versus language processing. To address this, we propose MAEP (Modality-Aware Expert Pruning), a training-free framework that decomposes expert importance by modality in a single forward pass. MAEP introduces (1) Delta-H Output ($ΔH$), a redistribution-aware metric measuring the MoE output change when an expert is removed, and (2) low-attention vision tokens as a vision-side pruning signal that is dataset-stable: its expert-importance ranking remains stable across different datasets. Text-side importance serves as a preservation signal while vision-side importance serves as a pruning signal, and experts are ranked globally across layers. Experiments on three MoE-based MLLMs across multimodal and text benchmarks show that MAEP outperforms modality-agnostic baselines on the majority of multimodal benchmarks while remaining competitive on text-only tasks, reducing the BF16 expert weight footprint by about 27 GB (about 47% of total memory) so that the full weight footprint of an approximately 58 GB model fits within a single 40 GB GPU. Code is available at https://github.com/nota-github/MAEP.

</details>

Abstract: Quantifying glomerular inflammation in kidney-transplant biopsies is traditionally performed on single whole-slide sections using the Banff "most-severe section" rule, despite the inherently three-dimensional nature of lesion distribution. This 2D based assessment might lead to instability when inflammation varies across slices. This paper reports a feasibility study examining the technical feasibility and clinical relevance of extending Banff scoring into three dimensions. To this end, we propose a 3D Banff lesion-scoring framework that aligns serial sections, reconstructs glomerular identities across depth, and integrates inflammatory-cell counts in 3D under three scoring strategies: Compatible, Volumetric, and Deduplicated Volumetric. In the experiments, glomerulitis (g-scores) was used as an example Banff metric and applied to multi-section renal allograft biopsies. Our findings indicate that 3D Banff g-scores are more consistent across sections and, under the semi-automatic setting, the Volumetric strategy correlates more strongly with clinical biomarkers than traditional 2D scores. These results show that 3D volumetric quantification offers promising added value, underscoring the potential benefit of 3D-aware Banff scoring for kidney transplant pathology.

</details>


### [139] [CLEAR: Color-Texture Latent Alignment for Single Image Reflection Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05969)
*Tu Vo,Sheir A. Zaheer,Chan Y. Park*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Single Image Reflection Removal (SIRR) seeks to recover the transmitted scene from a photograph captured through glass. Recent latent restoration methods use pretrained generative priors, but many recover detail by feeding high-frequency features from the degraded input back into the decoder. This works for degradations such as blur, noise, or haze, where input detail mostly belongs to the scene. It is less suitable for SIRR, where reflections add a second structured layer: directly reusing the input's skip connections can sharpen the image while also bringing reflection edges back. Latent compression also introduces color drift and fine-detail loss.
  We propose CLEAR (Color-Texture LatEnt Alignment for Single Image Reflection Removal), a deterministic, single-timestep, non-iterative latent framework that uses a pretrained latent diffusion backbone as a natural-image prior. A physics-guided separator organizes the prediction around the transmission, reflection, and residue formation model and outputs coarse layer latents in one forward pass. The Hybrid Feature Alignment Module (HFAM) then treats input-derived skips as contaminated layer-mixture features. It rectifies their high-frequency content with a clean-transmission-aligned color query, learned from the input features themselves under ground-truth feature supervision, keeping transmission-consistent texture while attenuating reflection-colored detail. CLEAR achieves the best average across fidelity and perceptual metrics on six real-world benchmarks and the lowest LPIPS on every dataset, with the largest gains on the hardest benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: For decades, diagnostic pathology has relied on thin two-dimensional (2D) tissue sections, despite biological tissues exhibiting inherently three-dimensional (3D) morphological organization. Because a single section captures only one focal plane, important morphological cues along the z-axis are inevitably discarded, limiting tissue representation for both human interpretation and computational analysis. In this work, we propose a generative framework that learns z-axis morphological priors from pathology z-stack data, offering a cost-effective alternative to dedicated volumetric imaging, and transfers this knowledge to conventional single-plane histopathology. Specifically, an image-to-video (I2V) latent diffusion model (LDM) is trained to synthesize a pseudo z-stack from a single 2D image, capturing plausible morphological variations across adjacent focal planes rather than reconstructing physical tissue volumes. To enable deployment on large-scale clinical datasets without native z-stack acquisition, we further introduce a prior-preserving mixture fine-tuning strategy that adapts the model to new domains while retaining the learned z-axis morphological prior. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the synthesized pseudo z-stacks exhibit realistic cross-plane morphological continuity and consistently improve downstream prostate cancer classification compared with conventional 2D representations. Our results suggest that learning transferable z-axis priors from limited z-stack data provides a practical way to enrich routine pathology images with complementary morphological information. Code: https://github.com/QuIIL/PathoS2VD.

</details>

Abstract: Video editors often work with large collections of fragmented footage and must select and organize relevant clips into a coherent story. However, many existing video editing and summarization methods assume a temporally ordered source video and aim to preserve its original narrative and temporal structure. In this paper, we introduce Clips2Story, a training-free, LLM-based pipeline that enables users to create keyword-specified short videos from a pool of video clips. Given a pool of clips and a user-specified keyword, we first extract text-based multimodal descriptors for each clip and retrieve the clips most relevant to the keyword. We then provide the textual descriptors of these candidate clips to an LLM, which selects and arranges them into an editable storyboard with connective narrations. Finally, we assemble the selected clips and retrieve accompanying visuals for the narrations to compose the output video. To evaluate the proposed pipeline, we construct a synthetic evaluation dataset from 100 long-form videos across five genres by segmenting each video into short clips using a scene-change detection model and shuffling the resulting clips to remove their temporal order. Through objective evaluation and qualitative examples, we demonstrate that Clips2Story can effectively retrieve keyword-relevant clips and compose coherent storyboards based on user-specified keywords. Human evaluation further shows that Clips2Story outperforms a strong baseline in terms of narrative coherence, keyword alignment, and overall realism.

</details>


### [142] [FedIA: Instance-Aware Cross-Modal Representations for Federated Prompt Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05994)
*Xuanjing Zhang,Qi Guo,Yanan Qiao,Yong Han,Junge Bo*

Abstract: Federated Prompt Learning (FPL) enables parameter-efficient adaptation of pre-trained Vision-Language Models across decentralized clients without exchanging raw data. To cope with cross-client heterogeneity, existing FPL methods typically pair a globally shared prompt with a client-specific local prompt. However, this design personalizes only at the client level: the same local prompt is applied to all samples of a client, and the global and local prompts are combined in an input-agnostic, fixed manner. Consequently, it overlooks the substantial visual variations among samples within the same client and cannot adjust the balance between shared and personalized knowledge to each input. To address these limitations, we propose Federated Prompt Learning with Instance-Aware Cross-Modal Representations (FedIA), an FPL framework that learns instance-aware cross-modal representations. Specifically, FedIA first uses global text prompts as client-invariant semantic anchors to select image patches most relevant to each candidate class, suppressing distracting regions. The selected visual evidence then conditions the generation of instance-aware local text representations. Finally, an adaptive global-local fusion module calibrates the contributions of local and global text representations for each image-class pair. In this way, FedIA shifts personalization in FPL from the client level to the instance level while preserving the semantic knowledge shared across clients. Extensive experiments under domain shift, pathological label skew, and Dirichlet-based label skew show that FedIA outperforms FPL baselines, with maximum accuracy gains of 1.44\%-4.37\% over the latest baseline across the three settings. Ablation studies and analyses further verify the complementary contributions of instance-aware representation generation and adaptive global-local fusion.

</details>

Abstract: Designing systems that attribute a generated image to its source model has lately become a major area of AI research. Most existing datasets and systems related to image source attribution are targeted towards the detection of architectural signatures of the source models. Distinct from this, focusing on stable diffusion, we introduce a novel task, IntrAA-SD, Intra-Architecture source Attribution for Stable Diffusion, where the source models to be differentiated share the same architectural backbone and differ only due to fine-tuning. The proposed task aligns with the emergence of fine-tuned generators having the same architecture, like Juggernaut XL and RealVis XL, which are fine-tuned versions of Stable Diffusion XL (SDXL). We provide a first-of-its-kind $140$K-image benchmark dataset containing images generated from different fine-tunes of SDXL, along with in-domain and out-of-distribution prompts. We also provide a baseline framework for IntrAA-SD, which leverages a style-conditioned Mixture-of-Experts network. It uses a frozen CLIP backbone to capture the image style and gate the MoE, which processes fine-tuned trace features from a high-resolution LoRA-adapted DINOv2 network. The fine-tuned models used, the prompting strategies considered, and the formation details employed in the novel dataset are elaborated. Our baseline intra-architecture source attribution method is evaluated in terms of attribution accuracy on our dataset, and ablation studies are also performed. Code and dataset is available at https://github.com/mallikagarg/IntrAA-SD.

</details>

Abstract: Motorized Two-Wheelers (MTWs) dominate personal transport across much of the Global South, yet remain largely overlooked in predictive perception research for autonomous driving and ADAS. We introduce MotoVerse, a benchmark for predictive perception from an MTW's ego-centric perspective. MotoVerse comprises 949 maneuver clips collected in dense, unstructured traffic and trajectory annotations for over 31,000 surrounding road agents, including over 16,000 MTWs, enabling two complementary tasks: Ego-Rider Maneuver Anticipation (ERMA) and Traffic Agent Trajectory Prediction (TATP). Each clip is further annotated with an MTW-specific challenge taxonomy comprising dynamic ego-motion, weak traffic structure and contextual occlusion, enabling challenge-aware evaluation. We benchmark established conventional prediction models, general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs), driving-specific VLMs and vision-language-action models. Our experiments reveal three key insights: (i) video backbones reach most of their full-observation accuracy from the first fifth of a maneuver, whereas zero-shot VLMs need most of the maneuver to be observed; (ii) zero-shot VLMs, whether general-purpose or driving-specific, largely remain close to simple baselines on both tasks; a preliminary LoRA study shows that adaptation lifts anticipation sharply but leaves trajectory prediction behind conventional models; and (iii) the rider's own motion drives forecasting difficulty: for every model, including a static baseline, errors are highest during left turns and rise under dynamic ego-motion. We believe MotoVerse will serve as a challenging benchmark for safer and more robust predictive AI for intelligent two-wheelers. The dataset and evaluation code are publicly available at https://github.com/VarunIIITH/MotoVerse.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied video world models predict scene changes caused by robot actions, yet generated object responses may remain inconsistent with task intent and contact dynamics. Critical responses in embodied scenes often concentrate in local regions and specific time intervals. Inspired by chain-of-thought reasoning, we introduce ReactWorld, an embodied video generation model guided by structured interaction anticipation. In the generation backbone's feature space, an interaction response field predictor anticipates how the interaction will unfold, explicitly describing participant roles, future motion response intensity, and local motion changes. A response feedback adapter forms increments to object features from the difference between the object branch's outputs with predicted and zeroed local dynamics inputs. It also converts predicted displacements into positional biases for temporal attention. These two mechanisms guide object feature updates and information retrieval across frames, respectively. Through interaction anticipation and generative feedback, ReactWorld achieves an average score of 77.51 on RBench, outperforming PhysisForcing by 3.29 points with improvements in all nine categories. On EZS-Bench, it achieves a Domain score of 86.98 and an average score of 81.87.

</details>


### [146] [ChanSFormer: A Channel Agnostic Vision Transformer for Multi-Channel Cell Painting Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06015)

Abstract: High-content multi-channel imaging, from Cell Painting assays to remote sensing, is central to modern scientific pipelines. Because these channels are highly heterogeneous and experimental configurations frequently evolve, a practical vision backbone must be fundamentally channel-agnostic. Current channel-adaptive vision transformers rely on global self-attention with learnable channel embeddings, which can be computed only for previously known channels. Attempts to overcome this limitation of new channels either discard the embeddings, losing channel identity, or process each channel independently, thereby eliminating critical cross-channel interactions. To address this, we introduce ChanSFormer, an efficient Vision Transformer that replaces rigid embeddings with disentangled spatial-channel attention and individual channel CLS tokens. This architecture natively preserves both distinct channel identities and cross-channel information flow. Furthermore, the individual channel class token design unlocks more informative feature representations for self-supervised learning and enables representation-based channel sampling while remaining channel-agnostic. We evaluate ChanSFormer on two biology multi-channel datasets, CHAMMI, JUMP-CP and a satellite dataset, So2Sat. Experimental results show that ChanSFormer outperforms state-of-the-art methods by 3.93%-4.27% on JUMP-CP in classification accuracy. It also demonstrates exceptional cross-dataset transferability under a self-supervised setting, outperforming previous methods by up to 2.72% in accuracy in the same classification tasks. Furthermore, the disentangled attention reduces the quadratic complexity of channel-by-spatial sequence length of the global attention baseline, thus improving throughput by 55%-313%. Code is available at https://github.com/cvlab-stonybrook/ChanSFormer.

</details>

Abstract: A 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) reconstruction inherits arbitrary units from structure-from-motion, so it cannot be measured or used to place AR content until someone supplies a scale. Both standard recipes are fragile on real captures: on five indoor scenes with tape-measured ceilings, a quantile floor-to-ceiling heuristic errs by 27% and metric-depth alignment by 35-71% across three networks. We present METRIC-GS, a selective scale estimator for real indoor photographs: it reads the scale off the reconstruction's own semantic content and, just as importantly, refuses when the evidence is thin. A frozen 2D teacher labels Gaussians by visibility-aware voting; instance-level size priors for architectural and furniture classes (doors, tables, the floor-to-ceiling gap) are fused by a robust maximum-likelihood step; and an inlier-mass test turns thin evidence into an explicit abstention that doubles as a router to a heavyweight metric model. On 47 ScanNet++ scenes with laser ground truth, with a configuration frozen before public evaluation, the estimator alone cuts the heuristic's mean error from 21.1% to 16.6% (9.3% on the 28 scenes it acts on) at ~11 s per scene. MASt3R remains the more accurate standalone estimator (9.9%), but routing only our refusals to it matches running it everywhere with 60% fewer invocations; this selective hybrid is the configuration we advocate. Visibility-aware voting raises the scenes the estimator can act on from 16 to 28 at unchanged deployed accuracy. Applied unchanged to 13 phone and drone captures of our own, on which MASt3R fails its own registration gate 12 times, the system improves all five captures it acts on and degrades none. We also report that a current feed-forward metric model scores 2.3% on a benchmark inside its training mixture and 28-35% on captures it has never seen: a published metric-scale number is a best case, not a deployment estimate.

</details>

Abstract: Underwater spatial reasoning depends on object boundaries and geometric cues that are obscured by scattering, color distortion, and low visibility. Existing underwater resources capture these cues through images, annotations, and depth maps, but provide fragmented supervision for vision-language models. We present a spatial data engine that connects visual appearance with object-level geometry through enhancement, 2D and geometry-assisted question generation, and expert filtering. It produces 4.27M QA pairs across five capability groups. We use this corpus to train USI-Brain and introduce an Underwater Prior-guided Rectifier (UPR) that applies gated corrections to visual features. USI-Bench evaluates the resulting capabilities with 6,525 held-out QA pairs. On its 15 task metrics, USI-Brain and USI-Brain + UPR improve over Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct by 5.76 and 7.63 points on average, respectively, using signed differences on 100-point scales. Ablations show that geometry-assisted supervision improves depth and ordering by 4.8 and 6.1 points with UPR disabled, while UPR adds 18.0 points in scene description under matched training data. Both variants use raw images at evaluation. Across NautData, UWBench, and MarineEval, USI-Brain achieves the best or joint-best score on 28 of 32 metrics. Model weights and USI-Bench will be released at https://github.com/SKY-Zhu-91/USI-Brain.

</details>

Abstract: Universal image restoration aims to handle diverse degradations within a single model, but a reliable scaling strategy remains underexplored. Increasing backbone depth and width can lead to performance saturation, while degradation-aware specialization depends on accurate degradation estimation and is vulnerable to errors on out-of-distribution and real-world inputs. We propose UniRestorer++, a scalable framework based on multi-granularity degradation modeling. It progressively refines degradation groups and introduces group-specific experts, coupling capacity growth with specialization. Degradation-estimation-based routing selects a root-to-leaf path, while granularity-estimation-based routing chooses a level along that path to balance specialization and robustness. Spatially dense degradation representation maps further enable region-aware routing for spatially non-uniform corruptions. We develop CDD-50, a benchmark covering 50 degradation categories from single to penta-degradation. Experiments show approximately log-linear PSNR trends over the evaluated model-size range and stronger scaling gains on out-of-distribution and real-world data than vanilla MoE scaling. UniRestorer++ achieves state-of-the-art performance across benchmarks and demonstrates zero-shot generalization and few-shot transfer on real-world images. Code: https://github.com/mrluin/UniRestorer_plusplus

</details>

Abstract: In recent years, AIGC technologies are capable of generating hyper-realistic forged facial images, posing severe threats to facial security safety. To tackle these challenges, early Deepfake research primarily centered on real-fake classification. Recently, growing efforts have been devoted to the Deepfake Attribution (DFA) of generated content. Nevertheless, existing research on Deepfake detection and attribution faces saturated performance in binary classification, limited diversity in datasets and algorithms, and imperfect evaluation protocols, which severely impede practical application. To address these limitations, we propose a comprehensive deepfake detection and attribution benchmark named DeepfakeGenome (DFG). It contains 100 facial forgery algorithms and 2M images in total, achieving 4x to 100x larger than prior DFA benchmarks. We further design 4 protocols for practical evaluation, including a novel retrieval-based attribution paradigm. Unlike previous open-set evaluation metrics, the proposed retrieval metrics are more aligned with the real-world active defense situation of blacklist registration mechanisms. Based on these elaborate designs, we investigate the performance ceiling of deepfake attribution task. Over 2k experimental evaluations are conducted, and 10 insightful findings are derived. We believe this work can provide new insights into the DFA research field. The Code and Benchmark of DFG are avaliable on https://huggingface.co/spaces/DeepfakeGenome/DFG_Page.

</details>


### [151] [RI-Prune: Relation and Information Aware Visual Token Pruning for Multi-Image Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06047)

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) have recently been extended to handle multiple input images, but this extension dramatically increases the visual token budget, making visual-token pruning not merely an efficiency option but a practical necessity. Existing visual-token pruning methods, primarily designed for the single-image setting, rely on attention-derived saliency signals to estimate per-token importance. However, this attention-centric design transfers poorly to the multi-image regime: it causes substantial performance degradation on multi-image benchmarks, suffers from strong positional bias, and loses discriminative attention signals as the number of input images increases. To test whether attention-selected tokens are truly important for multi-image reasoning, we conduct two complementary causal probes Leave-One-In and Leave-One-Deletion, which measure token importance by restoring individual tokens to a zeroed visual sequence or removing them from the full sequence. Attention-based pruners provide only marginal gains over random selection, showing that attention is a weak proxy for visual-token importance in multi-image reasoning. Motivated by this gap, we propose Relation and Information Aware Pruning (RI-Prune), a training-free, attention-free pre-decoder selector for multi-image visual token pruning. RI-Prune scores each visual token using a z-normalized combination of three signals computed from projector outputs: query relevance, cross-image correspondence with compact per-image prototypes, and intra image representativeness. By integrating these signals, RI-Prune performs balanced per-image Top-K selection to preserve information across input images. Across multiple multi-image benchmarks and three LVLM backbones, RI-Prune achieves a stronger trade-off between token reduction and task performance than prior pruning methods, particularly under aggressive token budgets.

</details>

Abstract: Text-to-SVG systems are commonly evaluated by applying raster-image metrics to rasterised outputs, yet whether these scores capture fine-grained prompt faithfulness remains unclear. We introduce SVGFaith, a two-layer meta-evaluation framework in which the more faithful candidate of each pair is determined by construction or by human judgment. SVGFaith-Diagnostic contains 1,842 controlled contrastive pairs across eight capability axes, split evenly between two directions: two renderings compared against one prompt, and two descriptions compared against one rendering. SVGFaith-Arena contains 1,327 human-annotated comparisons of real generator outputs. On SVGFaith-Diagnostic, CLIP and three other image--text embedding metrics reach at least 0.789 pairwise accuracy on object identity but at most 0.672 on spatial relations and attribute binding, where chance is 0.5. We further introduce SVGFaith-Score, an interpretable pointwise metric. A domain-tuned decomposer turns each prompt into a dependency graph of atomic visual requirements, a visual question answering model scores whether each requirement is satisfied, and a requirement's score is discounted when its prerequisites score low. In addition to the overall score, the per-requirement scores show which requirements are judged unsatisfied and which inherit discounts from their prerequisites. SVGFaith-Score significantly outperforms all nine baselines, including a Qwen3.5-122B-A10B judge, on Arena and in both Diagnostic directions. Code and data: https://github.com/Yukinonooo/SVGFaith.

</details>

Abstract: Recent video world models allow users to explore generated environments through camera navigation. Continuous hand motion provides a fine-grained action channel for interacting with the people and objects encountered during exploration. We present TouchéWorld, a framework that integrates gesture-driven interaction into a camera-controlled video world model. Given an initial image and a scene-description prompt, the model generates video conditioned on temporally aligned camera and hand controls. We construct joint camera--hand training data from heterogeneous egocentric videos through annotation completion, consistency-based filtering, and temporal alignment. Building on a pretrained camera-controlled world model, we combine spatial hand-skeleton conditioning with numerical hand-motion features through parameter-efficient adaptation. Causal adaptation and few-step distillation support chunk-level online generation, allowing users to update their controls based on generated visual feedback. We further introduce TouchéBench to evaluate hand-motion following, camera--hand coordination, online interaction, and object response. Together, these components establish a framework for studying continuous exploration and hand-driven interaction within generative worlds.

</details>

Abstract: While single-image 3D texture generation has advanced significantly, conditioning texture synthesis on multi-view reference images remains largely under-explored, despite its necessity for resolving surface ambiguity in unobserved regions. In this paper, we propose RouTex, a novel multi-reference 3D texture generation framework based on in-context Diffusion Transformers (DiT). Our key insight is that native 3D Rotary Position Encoding (RoPE) inherently possesses spatial addressing capabilities. We introduce Positional Routing (PR) to reprogram 3D RoPE along spatial and semantic dimensions, establishing explicit reference-to-view correspondence without introducing additional parameters. To execute this routing efficiently, Dual-Granularity Attention (DGA) combines per-view local attention for fine texture fidelity with global joint attention over deduplicated references for cross-view consistency. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RouTex achieves new state-of-the-art performance in multi-reference 3D texture generation.

</details>

Abstract: Omni models answer questions about videos by jointly processing sampled frames and audio in one turn, with progress driven largely by scaling model size and omni training data. We ask whether a model needs to hear the audio directly at all, and present SleuthVLM, an agent harness built on a VLM that never hears the audio directly: speech will be transcribed into text, and the VLM will sleuth the video on its own without any predefined workflow. On four omni benchmarks with videos up to 90 minutes, SleuthVLM achieves the best average accuracy: \textbf{75.0%}, 2.6 points above Gemini 3.8 Flash, and it also tops the Video-MME-v2 leaderboard with \textbf{77.8%}; with an open-source 27B VLM, it beats all open-source omni models. We draw three findings: SleuthVLM beats a model that glances; hearing is not the bottleneck, agentic ability is; omni understanding needs better agentic ability, not just more omni data for modality alignment. So we provide a simple but effective starting point for doing omni video understanding. Omni understanding needs better agents, not just bigger models that hear.

</details>


### [156] [GeoBiaset: A Counterfactual Benchmark for Demographic Bias in World-Level Geolocalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06085)

Abstract: World-level image geolocalization is an increasingly important task, yet the biases of current models remain poorly understood. Prior work has identified coarse dataset imbalances noting, for instance, that some cities are absent from training sets and that models tend to perform better in wealthier regions. However, no one has yet examined which visual cues drive these disparities. To do so, we introduce GeoBiaset, the first benchmark designed to measure how geolocalization models are influenced by the apparent race of a person inserted into the image foreground. The dataset consists of 9,541 images, pairing existing geotagged background images with counterfactual edits spanning seven races, holding the ground-truth coordinates fixed. Controlled masking experiments show that occlusion and background drift do not fully explain geolocalization degradation. Alongside the benchmark, we introduce the Person-Origin Perturbation Indicators (POPI), novel metrics that quantify model resilience to these demographic perturbations by measuring whether predictions are pulled toward the region associated with the inserted group. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art geolocalization and multimodal large language models reveals a 16-47% drop in continent-level performance when a subject from a demographically mismatched group is inserted. A landmark versus no-landmark analysis and gradient-weighted heatmaps show that models preferentially attend to geographic landmarks when present, but shift attention to facial features in their absence.

</details>

Abstract: In this study, we focus on service robots that retrieve and transport everyday objects based on free-form instructions that may refer to visual features and scene text. This is challenging because it requires a multimodal understanding of the visual features and spatial relationships of objects, as well as scene text embedded in the environment. To address this, we propose Scene Text-Aware multimodal Retrieval for Everyday objects (STARE), which leverages visual and textual cues in response to free-form instructions. STARE introduces Crosslingual Visual Prompt to enhance scene text interpretation, and models the complex relationships between named entities in the instructions and their corresponding objects. We evaluate STARE using two novel benchmarks, GoGetIt and TextCaps-test, both of which consist of real-world indoor/outdoor images and instructions involving object manipulation and navigation. The results show that STARE outperforms all baseline methods on standard multimodal retrieval metrics. Furthermore, in physical experiments conducted under a zero-shot transfer setting, STARE achieves an overall success rate of 80%. Our project page and code are provided in the Supplementary Materials.

</details>


### [158] [Drift-adaptive Representation Preservation for Domain-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06093)
