Abstract: Online reinforcement learning offers an effective approach to precise laboratory manipulation, yet costly real-world demonstrations and limited access to dense process rewards hinder its practical application. We present TwinBridge, a digital twin-driven framework that supplies policy initialization and dense visual rewards for real-world online learning. We construct digital twins by combining 3D Gaussian splatting with physics simulation. Here, simulator states provide task events and geometric progress under physical prerequisites and ordering constraints, yielding rewards for policy pretraining with simulated demonstrations and reinforcement learning. We distill this supervision into a visual reward model. During real-world interaction, its predictions are combined with execution history to compute dense rewards for human-in-the-loop policy refinement. Across five real-world laboratory tasks, TwinBridge achieves a final mean autonomous success rate of 98% with at most 25 minutes of real-world interaction per task. Experiments show that dense rewards improve interaction efficiency over sparse rewards, and simulation-based policy initialization further accelerates learning.

</details>


### [321] [Beyond Infraction Scores: Failure-Boundary Profiles for Closed-Loop Evaluation of Driving and Robot Navigation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06453)

Abstract: Closed-loop driving benchmarks in CARLA rank agents by a driving score that multiplies route completion by an infraction penalty. This score does not distinguish an agent that stops in front of an obstacle from one that pushes past it. In a corridor closed by two parked cars, for example, one agent that drives through with contact averages 52.2 on the blocked widths, while the agents that stop average between 37.8 and 40.6. We propose failure-boundary profiles, an evaluation in which each scenario family varies one measured factor, such as corridor width or the time gap in oncoming traffic, and each variant is certified from measurement as one where the agent should proceed or one where it should stop. Along this axis we estimate two curves per agent, commitment (the probability of attempting the passage) and competence (the probability of passing without contact), and locate where each crosses one half, with intervals that account for the simulator's run-to-run variance. The certified labels also give a summary metric, decision accuracy, which credits clean passes on passable variants and stops on blocked ones. We evaluate seven driving agents on seven CARLA families. The rule-based expert reaches a decision accuracy of 0.74 and TFv6, the highest-scoring learned agent, 0.60. Agents with similar driving scores fail in different ways, and agents that reliably stop at a blocked corridor still rarely wait for a gap in oncoming traffic. We also apply the method, unchanged, to a warehouse robot in Isaac Sim. The navigation policy we test stops at blocked gaps in only one of three families, and two fine-tuned versions of it pass more gaps cleanly but no longer stop at all.

</details>


### [322] [Robocasa-Mem: Benchmarking Environment and Process Memory in Long-Horizon Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06475)

Abstract: In the real world, mobile manipulation robots must perform complex object interactions and make multi-step decisions, often relying on task-relevant information that is no longer observable. However, existing benchmarks provide limited evaluation of such memory-dependent decision-making in mobile manipulation. To address this gap, we introduce RoboCasa-Mem, a new benchmark comprising nine mobile manipulation tasks that require agents to use interaction history at key decision points. Specifically, the tasks are grouped into two categories of memory: One is environment memory, which captures spatial locations, object attributes, and object relationships. The other is process memory, which captures single-task progress and multi-task scheduling. Particularly, we introduce Behavior-Conditioned Evaluation. For tasks with multiple valid execution paths, instead of fixing a single target or trajectory, the evaluator derives later task constraints from the robot's earlier actions. This allows us to evaluate whether later behavior is consistent with the robot's own interaction history while reducing shortcuts based on fixed target assignments. We provide 1,800 successful demonstrations for policy training. We also develop a baseline on our benchmark, namely Hierarchical Memory-Augmented Policy(HMAP). Its high-level planner uses structured entity and process memories to generate subtasks. Its low-level VLA controller uses subgoal-conditioned queries to incorporate VGGT-Omega geometry into action generation. Together, RoboCasa-Mem and HMAP baseline support systematic evaluation of memory-dependent behavior and the development of memory-aware mobile manipulation policies.

</details>

Abstract: Coordinated whole-body control of humanoid robots remains challenging due to high-dimensional coupled dynamics and substantial physical heterogeneity across joint degrees of freedom (JDoFs). Existing multi-agent reinforcement learning methods either employ coarse agent partitions or directly apply generic heterogeneous-agent algorithms, while hierarchical controllers commonly optimize high-level planning and low-level execution independently, leading to uncoordinated motion. In this paper, we propose H^2MARL, a hierarchical heterogeneous multi-agent reinforcement learning framework with two key mechanisms. We first formulate humanoid whole-body control using a JDoF-level physical-heterogeneity-aware policy factorization, where each torque-actuated JDoF is conditioned on an explicit descriptor of its actuation, kinematic, and dynamic properties. Moreover, we develop a closed-loop policy-executor co-optimization mechanism that jointly adapts the high-level pose planner and the low-level joint executor using shared interaction trajectories, thereby reducing the distribution mismatch between planning and execution. Extensive experiments in MuJoCo and Isaac Gym demonstrate that H^2MARL consistently improves locomotion stability, gait coordination, and motion quality over homogeneous, coarse-grained, and independently optimized alternatives.

</details>


### [324] [$A^3$-VLA: Automatic Perception-Guided Attention Alignment for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06447)

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) enable robots to perform manipulation tasks by leveraging Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to process visual observations and language instructions. However, the action prediction objective itself lacks fine-grained task-specific perceptual supervision, making it prone to relying on task-irrelevant visual shortcuts, leading attention away from target objects and key interaction areas, thereby limiting its operational performance and generalization ability. To address this, we propose Automatic Perception-Guided Attention Alignment for Robot Manipulation ($A^3$-VLA), a framework that derives task-specific visual guidance from the manipulation process itself. Specifically, $A^3$-VLA first generates heuristic candidate regions for target objects and the robot from interaction-induced visual changes, and refines them with a visual segmentation model offline. These regions serve as explicit perceptual guidance through two complementary objectives: an attention allocation loss that encourages sufficient focus on task-critical regions when language or robot state tokens query the visual input, and a contrastive learning loss that aligns the representations of candidate target regions with language instructions while separating them from task-irrelevant regions. Extensive experiments on simulated and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that $A^3$-VLA consistently improves multiple VLA backbones, with an average success rate gain of 6.5% - 25.2% across benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid soccer requires a robot to seamlessly combine agile locomotion, ball manipulation, and impact-rich contact dynamics across a continuous, dynamic behavioral spectrum. Yet existing approaches either cover only a limited set of skills, or forego motion priors entirely and produce unnatural behaviors, leaving the combination of a unified skill set and human-like motion quality unaddressed. We present soccerbot, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework that resolves both limitations through a structured latent motion prior distilled from large-scale human soccer motion capture data. By constraining policy learning within this dynamically plausible motion manifold, a single skill-conditioned policy naturally learns to perform moving, dribbling, trapping, and kicking in a unified formulation, while inherently producing human-like motion and fluid transitions during commanded skill switching. Augmented by robustness-oriented simulation design, soccerbot transfers zero-shot to the Unitree G1 humanoid robot, demonstrating agile, natural, and robust execution of the full soccer skill repertoire under real-world conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Ground robots operating in temperate forests suffer from systematic LiDAR occlusions caused by tree trunks, creating persistent blind regions that degrade traversability estimation. In this paper, we propose FOSCo-Net, a self-supervised voxel completion framework that learns to reconstruct occluded geometry from a single scan using temporally accumulated observations as automatic supervision, and we evaluate cylindrical and Cartesian ego-centric grids with multiple decoder designs. FOSCo-Net consistently improves reconstruction quality and traversal safety, with a gated skip decoder achieving the most reliable performance and significantly reducing false obstacle predictions compared to baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Controlling the shape profile of soft trunk robots extends their reach into complex regions of the workspace and enhances their use in practical applications. Recent advances in soft robot design have enabled soft trunk robots to achieve greater flexibility in conforming to complex shapes. Specifically, trunk robots whose geometries are designed as helicoids have been shown to enable extreme shape-changing capabilities. Their compliance makes them highly nonlinear, which in turn makes shape control difficult. On similar tendon-driven trunk robots, model-based controllers based on the theory of adiabatic spectral submanifolds (aSSMs) have achieved high accuracy in closed-loop end-effector tracking. We extend this approach by learning an aSSM model of the full body of a trimmed-helicoid soft trunk robot from data and use this in a model predictive control (MPC) scheme to demonstrate a range of shape control tasks on hardware. On closed-loop dynamic shape regulation, our method outperforms the available linear baselines by a factor of 10. In human-in-the-loop interactive demos, our method completes section-by-section shaping and balances a tennis ball for over 25 seconds, while the linear baselines fall short. A model-free reinforcement learning policy trained for 12 hours on hardware fails on the same balancing task. These results establish aSSM-based MPC as a practical route to full-body shape control on real soft hardware.

</details>


### [328] [SCRUB: Contact-Based Surface-Frame Estimation for Force-Motion Control with an Armless Quadruped](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06649)
*Sourav Selvaraj,Juan Pablo Espinosa Chessal,Mahdi Agheli*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Wiping, sanding, and inspection robots press a sliding tool on a surface with controlled force. Controlling that force needs the surface normal, which is unknown on real-world, unmapped surfaces. Surface-normal estimators can help estimate the surface normal, but they either need a friction model or assume that the instantaneous tool velocity lies along the surface; however, walking violates this assumption. We propose SCRUB, a contact-based surface-frame estimator, which lets an armless quadruped slide a body-mounted tool along near-vertical surfaces in the absence of known, axis-aligned, or vision-predicted normals. SCRUB's predictive controller presses and slides the tool using a Kalman-filter estimate of the normal from two cues: 1) A force cue measures the estimate's tilt across the motion. If friction directly opposes the motion, the cue is zero exactly when that tilt is zero, for any friction coefficient. However, if the cue's sliding direction is wrong, friction appears as a false tilt. 2) A velocity cue measures tilt along the motion. Walking and pressing move the tool in and out. SCRUB therefore subtracts the commanded press rate from the tool velocity, averages the result over each gait cycle, and treats this mean as lying along the surface. On Unitree Go1 hardware at nominal speed and load, SCRUB completed every wall traverse and column orbit. No orbit completed with either cue removed, without gait averaging, or with a fixed normal.

</details>

Abstract: A robot action can satisfy shape and range constraints yet carry the wrong semantics: axes may be permuted, signs inverted, rotations rescaled, or gripper polarity reversed. We study learned state-action-effect relations as offline contracts for detecting these mismatches and ranking their likely fields. Clean held-out transitions calibrate alarms; a fixed inverse vocabulary supports closed-set localization. On ten LIBERO Spatial tasks comprising 432 source episodes, a capacity-controlled comparison at 2.5% empirical clean false-positive rate gives 46.1% recall for a ridge forward residual, 61.0% for a four-component joint Gaussian mixture, and 65.7% for the exact conditional of that same mixture. The joint score retains a 14.9 percentage-point advantage over ridge, while conditioning improves recall by a further 4.7 points. Joint scoring instead has higher localization: 95.8% versus 94.6%. These matched-rate comparisons are retrospective diagnostics, not deployment calibration guarantees. Earlier Object/Goal results broaden tasks within the LIBERO convention; DROID, all three HYDRA tasks, short temporal faults, small offsets, exact repair, and simulated mitigation fail their respective gates, while xArm contributes one task only. The evidence supports a bounded offline diagnostic and a detection-localization tradeoff, rather than joint-density superiority or cross-robot safety.

</details>


### [330] [Compounding Physical RSI: Turning Execution Failure into Inherited Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06654)
*Yixuan Yang,Qiang Chen,Run Shao,Shuwei Dong,Wenhao Li,Yunpeng Wang,Wenhan Fang,Haomiao Wu,Xingyu Wang,Lichen Man,Rong Zhang,Xuezhi Cui,Yuan Liu,Bingyan Lu,Jiawei Ma,Xiaoyang Ma,Zhemin Sun,Zhuorui Yao,Yutong Dong,Yuxuan Zhao,Yichong Mu,Jiabao Jiang,Wenxuan Deng,Junling Zhou,Yi Chen,Deyu Zhang,Yiming Yan,Yige Yuan,Shan You,Fei Wang,Haifeng Li,Hongyan Xu,Yichao Cao,Xiu Su*

Abstract: Generalist robot policies are trained on static offline datasets, yet physical manipulation unfolds sequentially across stages. Because a weakness in a downstream stage remains unobservable until the policy passes earlier ones, each resolved bottleneck exposes the next hidden failure mode. This staged structure calls for Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI), in which a policy iteratively executes, diagnoses failures, and updates itself. Moreover, because task completion behaves like a product of stage-level pass rates, recursive gains can multiply across generations. In physical environments, three bottlenecks obstruct compounding: terminal execution feedback rarely localizes the stage that caused a failure, failed trajectories do not supply the corrective action, and updating shared parameters can degrade previously mastered skills.
  We propose Compounding Physical RSI, a self-evolving policy framework that turns execution failures into heritable capability. First, automated capability-gap assessment traces failed rollouts to the task stage where progression breaks down and formulates a structured improvement objective. Second, conditioned on this objective, our remedy stage curates validated, stage-relevant experience and refines the policy via behavior-regularized offline reinforcement learning, without requiring costly online rollouts at each optimization step. Third, to safeguard existing skills, our retention stage constructs weight-space candidates between the inherited reference policy and the refined policy, evaluates them across the task suite, and promotes the best candidate as the reference state for the next generation.
  Evaluated across all 42 tasks of the RoboDojo-Sim benchmark, CSU-AI-07 ranks first on the leaderboard with a three-seed mean Overall Score of 26.15 and Success Rate of 20.70%, outperforming the next-best entry by 5.92 points and 5.82 percentage points.

</details>


### [331] [Which Physical Tactile Cues Matter for Contact-Rich Imitation Learning? A Controlled Real-Robot Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06673)

Abstract: Vision-based tactile sensors capture multiple aspects of physical interaction within a single image stream, including contact appearance, geometry, indentation depth, and tangential deformation. However, existing visuo-tactile policies often directly process tactile observations as images, making it difficult to determine which physical cues contribute to policy learning. This paper presents a controlled real-robot study that compares physically interpretable tactile signals using the LeRobot platform, enhanced with our visuo-tactile perception system. From the same tactile stream, we derive tactile RGB images, normal/depth maps, and marker-flow fields, representing contact appearance, geometry, and tangential deformation, respectively. Using identical task demonstrations and the same policy backbone, we evaluate the individual and combined contributions of these tactile cues. We further compare tactile encoders and visuo-tactile fusion strategies. Evaluation across 720 real-robot trials yields four key findings: (1) tactile perception substantially improves the overall success rate from 57.8% to 78.9%, although its contribution varies across task stages; (2) marker flow is the most effective individual cue, achieving a success rate of 74.4% and playing a particularly important role in regulating established contact; (3) cue-factorized encoding consistently improves performance, highlighting that different physical cues require tailored processing techniques; and (4) although the effectiveness of tactile fusion is task-dependent, cross-attention offers a lightweight solution with performance comparable to that of pretrained tactile encoders.

</details>


### [332] [Where Is VLA Inference Time Spent? Bottlenecks and Optimization Interactions Across GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06710)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models map camera observations and language instructions to robot actions. However, their large backbones and repeated action generation can make inference take longer than the robot's real-time requirements for its next action. To reduce the load of VLA computations, researchers have proposed multiple optimization approaches, including quantization and pruning. These optimizations can be combined with other general optimizations such as better kernel compilation or using CUDA graphs instead of the eager mode. However, most of these optimization results are usually reported on a single GPU type, typically for a few VLA models, with no analysis of what the real bottlenecks are for a model on a particular hardware or how these optimizations would generalize beyond the reported results. In this paper, we provide a comprehensive measurement study of seven families of inference optimization methods, from CUDA graphs and compilation to token pruning and quantization, on seven state-of-the-art VLAs evaluated on four GPU families ranging from a datacenter H100 to an embedded Jetson Thor. We analyse how CPU launch overhead, memory bandwidth, and kernel efficiency jointly shape optimization gains, and how applying one method changes the benefit of another. From this analysis, we derive VLA-Triage, guidelines for stacking optimizations under latency budgets. The guidelines identify which stages can provide the required savings and which combinations need testing in the deployed execution mode.

</details>


### [333] [Dexterous Robotic Hands in the Foundation-Model Era: A Full-Stack Survey of Hardware, Data, Cross-Embodiment Transfer, Learning, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06724)

Abstract: Dexterous robotic hands are increasingly coupled to large-scale human data and foundation models, yet progress in hardware, representation learning and evaluation is often assessed separately. This survey reviews work from January 2024 to early November 2026, with earlier studies included to establish technical lineages. We organize the literature into five interdependent layers: hardware; data collection and curation; cross-embodiment transfer; learning and foundation models; and evaluation and applications. Human hand-object understanding and tactile sensing provide two cross-cutting perspectives. A system-level architecture, comparative tables and five case analyses connect design choices across these layers. We distinguish within-study experimental comparisons from heterogeneous corpus statistics, vendor specifications and company-reported results. Three conclusions emerge. First, the value of human data depends on kinematic, visual and contact alignment, not recording volume alone. Second, shared action representations and foundation policies do not remove embodiment-specific constraints on force, latency and contact. Third, task completion is insufficient to establish physical correctness or deployment readiness. Evidence remains limited for zero-shot transfer of contact-rich skills between morphologically different hands, long-duration reliability, and cross-sensor tactile generalization. We identify eleven open problems and propose reporting interfaces and controlled comparisons that can make full-stack progress more measurable. The review is a structured narrative synthesis rather than a pooled performance ranking or an exhaustive systematic review.

</details>

Abstract: Coding agents powered by models such as GPT-6-Astra connect spatial reasoning with executable robot behavior, raising the question of how such agents can produce lasting improvements in robot capabilities from physical interaction. In this work, we study embodied recursive self-improvement (RSI) through real-world exploration, code refinement, and deployment using PyRUA-REAL, a framework in which coding agents retain interaction experience as persistent memory and reusable executable skills that inform subsequent exploration and improvement. Our empirical study places physical manipulation at the center of the improvement process, examining how agents collaborate, what kinds of execution feedback support effective code refinement, and how task specifications and previously acquired skills influence robustness and task transfer. We further investigate the efficiency and capability of the resulting programs when deployed without involving an online agent for decision-making. Across eight real-world tasks, PyRUA-REAL achieves an average success rate of 91.1%. In complementary evaluation on 42 tasks from the RoboDojo benchmark, it achieves an average success rate of 84.4%. This study provides empirical guidance for turning physical exploration into reusable robot skills that support continued improvement and robust, efficient deployment.

</details>

Abstract: Code-as-policy agents often use execution feedback to retry failed programs. Additional sampling and task guidance can also improve success, leaving the contribution of failure-specific feedback unclear. We study this question in simulated robot manipulation. A fixed lookup selects feedback templates using failure labels and the API variant. A frozen code model generates a fresh program after each scene reset. On the same 252 initial failures, Qwen2.5-Coder-7B recovers 85.3% within five retries, compared with 68.3% for resampling with the same retry cap. The advantage persists under fixed total-token budgets. Feedback from other episodes and fixed task guidance yield similar recovery point estimates, with no detected benefit from matching the coarse failure label. In a separate study, a fixed guided prompt used from the first attempt achieves an observed overall success rate similar to that of template-based feedback. Template-based feedback also lowers recovery on a nut-insertion task requiring a rim grasp, consistent with an incompatible center-grasp instruction. Evaluations of robot self-repair should include resampling, mismatched feedback, and concise, task-compatible static guidance as baselines.

</details>


### [336] [VABAR: A Variational Automation Benchmark for Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06881)

Abstract: For robots to operate reliably in commercial and industrial applications, they must persistently perform specific tasks with known variations in object shape and pose. We call this Variational Automation (VA) to differentiate these settings from fixed automation. Inspired by recent papers on Agentic Robotics, where pretrained LLM and VLM coding agents combine interpretable robot programming with the open-world adaptability of model free policies, we propose a general set of agentic robotics benchmark design criteria that includes accessibility, repeatability, diversity, relevance to agentic robotics and practical applications, physical realizability, resistance to benchmark saturation, and computational efficiency. We instantiate these principles in six manipulation task classes spanning industrial, medical, and infrastructure applications: tool sorting, tool hanging, gear meshing, syringe packing, cable insertion, and cable routing. We introduce the Variational Automation Benchmark for Agentic Robotics (VABAR), an open, easily replicable, simulation-based manipulation benchmark to systematically evaluating agentic robotics systems. Built fully on open-source tools, VABAR features detailed implementations using the Newton physics engine, standardized task variations, difficulty tiers, success metrics, and a library of 33 generalizable skills with human-authored programmatic reference solutions. We provide initial baseline evaluations using an open agentic manipulation harness, Graph-as-Policy (GaP), and Isaac Lab Arena.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid robots have the potential to automate a wide range of physical tasks. Progress toward generalist humanoid autonomy, however, remains constrained by a data bottleneck: mapping pixels and free-form language to low-level joint commands requires paired supervision, and no corpus of such data exists at sufficiently large scale. Some approaches rely on human teleoperation and wearable capture devices, which require at least an hour of human time to produce an hour of data. We present HALO, a humanoid autonomy system powered by a pipeline that synthesizes vision-language-motion data across a thousand procedurally generated homes. The corpus scales with compute rather than human labor. HALO acquires a repertoire of physical skills, including sitting on chairs, navigating to named objects, stepping sideways through half-open doors, crouching and crawling beneath tables, and pushing boxes. It orchestrates these skills to solve composite, long-horizon tasks. Without ever seeing a real home during training, HALO transfers zero-shot to the physical world, achieving a 91% success rate across 360 trials in six unmapped real-world environments. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-halo/ for code and videos.

</details>

Abstract: We present cuRoboV2-Fast, a GPU-accelerated planner that reduces both planning latency and execution time for collision-free, payload-aware, torque-limited motions. B-spline trajectory optimization reduces the required iterations, while accelerated robot computations and differentiable inverse dynamics reduce refinement cost and accommodate changing payloads. TimeOpt optimizes traversal speed along the fixed path under sampled velocity, acceleration, jerk, and torque constraints, exploiting local spline support to solve banded Newton systems efficiently on the GPU. With a 3,kg payload, cuRoboV2-Fast achieves 4.4 ms mean planning time on RTX 5090, 14.7x faster than cuRoboV2. Our method on Jetson AGX Orin plans faster than cuRoboV1 + TOPPRA on RTX 5090. A stereo-camera-based deployment on YAM achieves 5.93 s mean completed pick-and-drop cycles, 34.6% shorter than cuRoboV2. Abstract: This paper presents a probabilistic framework for the direct, data-driven synthesis of the stochastic linear- quadratic output-feedback control for linear systems sub- ject to process and measurement noise. Using delayed input-output measurements, we derive a lifted parameter- ization together with an equivalent lifted linear quadratic cost, enabling controller synthesis without explicitly identi- fying the lifted dynamics. Two complementary closed-loop formulations, based on trajectory and covariance represen- tations, are then developed, whose uncertainties depend only on the realized but unknown lifted noise. We char- acterize the relative performance of the two formulations in finite-data regimes. High-probability uncertainty bounds are developed for realized noises, leading to a convex optimization solution to the problem, whose feasibility cer- tifies probabilistic mean-square stability (p-MSS) and per- formance guarantees. To reduce conservatism, Gaussian zonotopes are used to refine uncertainty sets by elimi- nating noise realizations inconsistent with measured data, and a novel intersection rule enables the consistent fusion of data-driven and physics-informed information. These components are integrated into an adaptive framework that unifies indirect (identification-based) and direct (control- oriented) data-driven linear-quadratic output-feedback con- trol. Unlike existing direct data-driven LQR methods that verify stability a posteriori, our by-construction approach achieves robust stability alongside reduced conservatism and superior performance. Simulation results show that the proposed controller approaches the ideal optimal solution while outperforming existing data-driven methods under noisy measurements.

</details>

Abstract: Safe navigation in cluttered environments requires a safety representation that captures the available motion space along the planned path. A single local safe region may restrict progress through narrow passages and continuous turns. This paper presents a planning-guided look-ahead safety corridor framework for barrier-state-based navigation. Local safe convex polytopes are constructed around collision-free seeds along the forward reference path. Their facet constraints are combined using softmin, and the local functions are fused using softmax with entropy-based adaptive compensation. The resulting safe set remains within the union of the local polytopes while reducing the shrinkage caused by fixed compensation. A Poisson boundary-value problem then defines a differentiable interior safety field while preserving the prescribed corridor boundary. This field is embedded into the robot dynamics through one geometric barrier state. We establish sufficient conditions for corridor invariance based on barrier-state boundedness and active-corridor validity. Navigation simulations cover cluttered layouts, narrow passages, and moving obstacles. A differential-drive ground vehicle further demonstrates navigation through narrow passages and among obstacles absent from the prior map.

</details>


### [341] [Decentralized Impedance Specifications for Grid-Connected Converters: a Sequence-Domain Gershgorin Method, and a Frequency-Wise Framework for Combining Different Criteria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05683)

Abstract: In complex grid scenarios, with many power electronics (PE) converters connected, having specifications for converters can ease the design process. A practical approach is to impose the specifications on the converter's small-signal impedance in the frequency domain. This is advantageous since impedance is a physical property that can be measured. This paper focuses on providing decentralized impedance specifications for each converter connected to an ac bus. With decentralized specifications, each converter has its own specifications, which are independent of other converters' impedance. This enables a competitive multi-vendor environment while guaranteeing system stability. Existing methods of imposing decentralized specifications, based on the small-gain, passivity and Gershgorin theorems, are formulated within a common frequency-domain framework. Building on this, two new solutions that reduce the conservativeness of the specifications are proposed. First, Gershgorin-based decentralized specifications are formulated in the modified sequence domain. Second, a framework for combining different specification types across frequency ranges is developed and mathematically proven. The methods are compared on examples covering sub-synchronous and harmonic-range interactions of grid-following converters, grid-forming converters, and a mixed grid-forming/grid-following system, using analytical models, black-box impedance measurements, and time-domain responses obtained from simulations and an experimental setup.

</details>


### [342] [Continuous-Discrete Dual-Domain Co-Design for Optimized Digital Control of Power Converters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05910)

Abstract: Optimizing digital control for power converters typically involves designing continuous-time controllers and discretizing them via Euler, Tustin, or Generalized Bilinear Transform (GBT) methods. However, the intrinsic coupling between continuous-domain control parameters and discrete-domain implementation parameters remains underexplored, often leading to suboptimal performance. To bridge this gap, this paper first establishes a Non-Uniqueness Theorem, elucidating the coupling mechanism between dual-domain parameters. Based on this, two co-design methodologies are proposed to derive optimized parameter sets: the Explicit Frequency-Domain Analytical Method (EFDAM) and the Implicit Computer-Aided Search Method (ICASM). The EFDAM derives closed-form solutions by maximizing the crossover frequency and resonant magnitude via logarithmic frequency stability criteria, while minimizing discretization errors using Taylor-series bounds. The ICASM formulates the search for optimized parameters as a constrained multi-objective optimization problem, balancing bandwidth, resonant magnitude, and fundamental magnitude. To solve ICASM efficiently, two algorithms are introduced: the Multi-Start COBYLA (MS-COBYLA), which employs Latin Hypercube Sampling for physics-informed initialization to escape local optima, and the PSO-COBYLA hybrid algorithm, which couples global particle swarm exploration with local refinement. Experimental results on a 5-kW single-phase inverter with a Proportional-Integral-Resonant (PIR) regulator validate that the proposed methods outperform the conventional benchmark, achieving a 43.3\% reduction in the peak-to-peak current ripple measured at the zero-active-power operating point and an 11.7\%--35.9\% decrease in THDi across various harmonic profiles.

</details>

Abstract: Impedance-based eigenvalue sensitivity provides a practical way to relate converter parameters to poorly damped system modes, but tuning becomes nontrivial when one parameter improves one target mode while degrading another. This paper introduces a conflict-aware tuning strategy for simultaneous damping enhancement of multiple modes. The method converts impedance-derived eigenvalue sensitivities into normalized damping-ratio sensitivities, diagnoses opposing modal tuning directions, and selects either one parameter or a coordinated pair at each iteration. A low-dimensional max--min problem maximizes the predicted damping of the worst target mode subject to parameter limits, protected-mode constraints, and implementation-specific feasibility requirements. Each accepted update is verified on the full system model and the sensitivities are recomputed. Studies on a four-bus system and a modified IEEE 14-bus system demonstrate the value of pairwise coordination, including a case in which single-parameter tuning stops before the predefined damping target is reached.

</details>

Abstract: Oscillatory power components in AI data-center demand can produce large frequency deviations when they coincide with vulnerable grid modes. Mode-aware hybrid energy storage systems (HESSs) can selectively suppress these components, but their effectiveness degrades when the grid mode changes while the control target remains fixed. This paper proposes a closed-loop grid-mode-adaptive HESS control framework that integrates measurement-based modal identification with mode-selective control. A sliding-window N4SID estimator identifies the local grid dynamics from grid-side power and frequency measurements, and the resulting natural-frequency estimate is used to retune the modal suppression branch assigned to the supercapacitor. The framework is evaluated under a grid-mode shift from 0.50 to 1.35Hz while maintaining equal physical damping and approximately equal load excitation at both frequencies. Compared with the fixed-target controller, the adaptive method reduces the post-change grid-side modal power component from 8.78 to 2.02MW, corresponding to a 76.94% reduction, and substantially lowers the resulting frequency deviation.

</details>

Abstract: Large data center projects introduce a distinct transmission planning uncertainty because of their large scale, discrete project realization nature, and strong locational impacts on the transmission network. On the one hand, conventional deterministic treatments can either overbuild the network by assuming that all candidate projects materialize; on the other, they can leave the system underprepared by prematurely screening out projects. This paper develops a project-realization-based framework for systematically modeling, planning, and evaluating transmission expansion under uncertain data center realization. Candidate data center projects are represented through alternative realization scenarios and associated probabilities, enabling different planning strategies to be modeled and quantitatively assessed under a common framework. Within this framework, numerical case studies demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework and the benefits of stochastic planning approach. The results show that stochastic planning can use realization probabilities to hedge multiple plausible futures, providing a more balanced tradeoff between transmission investment and reliability exposure than deterministic approaches under correctly specified and misspecified realization probabilities.

</details>

Abstract: We study the coordination of primary and secondary frequency regulation by grid-forming battery energy storage systems in islanded microgrids. We formulate the coordination as a multi-period bilevel program, in which the upper level schedules secondary regulation setpoints and each lower level computes primary regulation decisions. Using the lower-level Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, we obtain an exact single-level reformulation with complementarity constraints. To address the numerical difficulties introduced by these constraints, we develop a continuation method that couples the complementarity relaxation parameter with the interior-point barrier parameter and warm-starts successive stages. Under the stated boundedness assumptions and with vanishing subproblem residuals, we prove that every accumulation point is feasible and satisfies a first-order stationarity condition for the reformulated problem (C-stationarity). Numerical results for an islanded microgrid show that the proposed method reduces solution time by 89.3% and peak frequency deviation by 0.53% relative to conventional Scholtes relaxation.

</details>

Abstract: Max-pressure (MP) traffic signal control provides a decentralized mechanism for urban networks, but conventional pressure states do not directly capture the propulsion burden generated by signal-induced stop-and-go traffic. Eco-driving, by contrast, typically relies on prespecified signal information and optimizes vehicle trajectories separately from the feedback among vehicle motion, traffic states, and signal decisions. This paper develops Ecological Max-Pressure (Eco-MP), a closed-loop framework that integrates ecological MP signal control with MP-adaptive ecological driving. At the signal layer, connected-vehicle observations are aggregated into a common movement state based on cumulative positive propulsion work, and the resulting upstream--downstream ecological pressure determines phase selection in mixed petrol and electric traffic. At the vehicle layer, a rolling signal interface translates the evolving Eco-MP process into near-term signal information, while each connected and automated vehicle solves a powertrain-independent finite-horizon trajectory problem subject to vehicle-dynamic, car-following, and signal-compliance constraints to reduce unnecessary stopping and acceleration effort. Under full-state information, a finite-storage Lyapunov analysis establishes strong stability for admissible demands satisfying a strict supportability condition and extends the result to the coupled signal--vehicle system. When ecological driving enlarges the admissible-flow sets, the coupled admissible demand region contains the signal-only region. Microscopic experiments on an Amsterdam arterial corridor show that, relative to queue-based MP, Eco-MP reduces mean positive propulsion work by 5.2%, vehicle delay by 20.8%, and stopping time by 39.0%. With only 15% of vehicles directly controlled, ecological driving further reduces positive propulsion work by 4.2% and stopping time by 19.6%......

</details>

Abstract: This paper introduces transversal passivity, a local dissipation property for regulating input and output signals to prescribed manifolds. Its supply rate is built from the transverse port errors induced by nearest-point projections, so that motion along the manifolds is not penalized. We give a necessary condition and a constructive sufficient condition for the property and relate it to existing passivity notions. For feedback interconnections through negative-adjoint linear channels, power conservation no longer cancels the transverse supply. The residual is governed by compatibility defects that depend only on the channels and the prescribed manifolds, so it can be assessed before any closed-loop analysis, and it falls into a hierarchy of exact compatibility, quadratic cancellation, and nonzero quadratic residuals. Since transversal storages are only positive semidefinite, port convergence is obtained by combining compact confinement, for instance by conserved quantities, with a dissipation margin, and we identify a geometric obstruction under which no quadratic margin is available near the feasible states. A federated learning case study with heterogeneous data shows that port convergence can be certified even though no server target is compatible with the interconnection, and that the obstruction arises for a natural choice of target.

</details>

Abstract: Goal-oriented communication protocols are explicitly designed to support the objectives of target applications, such as the monitoring and control of remote processes, while minimizing data traffic. This study considers a transmitter node that observes the environment state and sends it to a remote agent, which then performs control actions. This problem can be modeled as a remote Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP), a subclass of decentralized POMDPs. Existing studies limit transmissions through communication costs, but neglect the effect of channel impairments and non-ideal or delayed feedback. In this work, we study the optimal joint transmission and control policies over lossy forward channels without explicit acknowledgments. We introduce \emph{revealing} policies, under which the receiver's action, observed directly by the transmitter, discloses the outcome of the forward transmission, keeping the two agents synchronized without a feedback channel. We derive sufficient conditions for the optimality of time-invariant policies in this implicit feedback regime for general POMDPs, along with strictly weaker sufficient conditions in the special case of contextual bandits. Our simulations over a family of remote POMDPs show that these conditions are conservative, and revealing policies are optimal over a substantially wider parameter range than the conditions warrant.

</details>

Abstract: The rising penetration of inverter-based resources (IBRs) brings rich control dynamics that can amplify spatial differences in nodal frequency through frequency-voltage (F-V) coupling. Existing analyses often neglect voltage dynamics or only examine the aggregate frequency, leading to overoptimistic evaluation of frequency security. To address these drawbacks, this paper develops a unified model of synchronous generators (SGs), grid-following (GFL)-IBRs, and grid-forming (GFM)-IBRs based on the magnitude and phase dynamics of equivalent internal electromotive force (EMF). Integrating these source models with network equations yields a sparse implicit closed-loop model, which fully captures the device-network F-V coupling without transfer-matrix inversion. Then, the obtained nodal responses are decomposed into common and differential modes. Using this decomposition, coupling indices and pole-residue analysis reveal how F-V coupling can amplify spatial differences in nodal frequency and how source configuration and parameters modify this effect. Finally, case studies on the modified IEEE 39-bus and 118-bus test systems validate the proposed model and demonstrate its computational efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: This study estimates the simultaneous charging factor (SCF) of electric vehicles (EVs) in Phu Quoc City, Vietnam, using a Monte Carlo simulation. Three planning scenarios (2030, 2035, and 2040) are developed from population-based EV uptake assumptions and vehicle-specific charging probability profiles over 24 hours, reflecting distinct operating patterns of motorbikes, cars, taxis, and buses. For each vehicle class and scenario, 1,000 simulation runs are performed to compute SCF and its uncertainty, reported via 95% confidence intervals. Results indicate that SCF decreases as EV penetration increases, implying more temporally dispersed charging demand and a reduced likelihood of extreme coincidence. For electric buses, SCF declines from 0.76 (2030) to 0.59 (2040), and other vehicle groups show similarly decreasing trends across the horizon. These SCF estimates provide practical inputs for grid-aware charging infrastructure planning, including diversity factors for sizing distribution transformers and feeders, identifying peak-hour stress, and informing demand management measures such as time of use tariffs and basic smart charging controls.

</details>

Abstract: This book provides a comprehensive and systematic exploration of neural network-based intelligent control systems for aerospace applications, bridging foundational theory with practical implementation. It is structured into two primary parts: Part I covers model-based control design, detailing Feedback Error Learning (FEL), pseudocontrol strategies, and neural backstepping for strict-feedback systems, along with practical refinements such as learning rule modifications, composite learning, and discrete-time formulations, all grounded in rigorous Lyapunov stability analysis. Part II introduces model-free control, laying the groundwork for Reinforcement Learning and Approximate Dynamic Programming. Throughout, theoretical concepts are solidified through detailed case studies on quadrotor UAVs, fixed-wing aircraft (GTM), satellites, and hypersonic flight vehicles, complete with performance metrics, parameter sensitivity analyses, and discussions of actuator faults, input constraints, and wind disturbances, making the book an invaluable resource for students and practicing engineers seeking to design, simulate, and analyze intelligent flight control systems.

</details>

Abstract: The approximation of model predictive control (MPC) algorithms with neural network models has received significant attention in the power electronics research community, as a valuable tool to enable the real-time implementation of computationally expensive methods. Training machine learning models to imitate MPC algorithms typically follows a traditional supervised learning flow, with a static training data set generated through either uniform sampling or simulation. Training with uniformly distributed data allows models to maintain consistent performance throughout the entire operating space, but very small models may fail to achieve satisfactory results in the regions corresponding to expected operation. Conversely, simulation data can be used in training to obtain models that can accurately track certain trajectories but fail to achieve adequate performance in underrepresented regions. The present article proposes a method to combine the advantages of both methods, by training models on sampled data with importance weights obtained through the kernel density estimation of experimental data. This allows the training process to target specific regions of the operating space, and results in measurable performance improvements. The method is demonstrated through the imitation of a moving discretized control set MPC algorithm for a dual active bridge converter, and is validated through experimental results.

</details>

Abstract: The Cellular Potts Model has emerged as a standard framework for modeling multicellular systems and metastatic progression. Yet, computational extensions incorporating cell movement within spatial gradients of Extracellular Matrix (ECM), an important element of the tissue microenvironment, lack theoretical foundations. This work provides a system-theoretic formulation and analysis of Extended Cellular Potts Models (ECPMs) that explicitly couple tumor dynamics with ECM degradation. We reformulate the ECPM as an interconnected dynamical system: a stochastic process describing a network of locally interacting tumor cells coupled to a jump-linear system describing ECM evolution. By exploiting the sparse energy structure of the model, we characterize asymptotic ECM behavior, transition probabilities with uniform bounds, and global system convergence. In one-dimensional settings, we derive closed-form expressions for invasion time probabilities, revealing how mean invasion time depends on local ECM gradients, number of cells and their tendency to maintain a target volume. Numerical simulations validate theoretical predictions, establishing a principled foundation for analyzing haptotaxis-driven invasion in Potts-based models.

</details>

Abstract: What is the threshold of an excitable system, i.e. the criterion that distinguishes rest from excitation? Previous research has shown that this question is far from trivial to answer. This paper studies the threshold of a simple excitable primitive in which excitability arises from the positive feedback interconnection of a first-order high-pass filter and a static, strictly monotone and strictly convex activation function. This threshold is a simple inequality on the difference between the instantaneous input and the motif's internal memory. While this result may help neuro-engineers characterize spiking in biological and artificial neurons, we argue that excitability is not always a desirable property, and that our threshold may also be relevant to safety considerations in fragile systems with rate-induced tipping points. We illustrate our framework with three examples: the FitzHugh-Nagumo model of neuronal dynamics, a model of stop-loss cascades in the stock market, and a social contagion model.

</details>

Abstract: Fixed-order frequency-weighted H-infinity approximation is a nonconvex problem, and local optimization methods can be sensitive to initialization. We present extremal-phase-guided sequential convex optimization (EPGSCO) for stable fixed-order rational approximation. We introduce a novel phase-guidance technique motivated by the all-pass structure underlying optimal Hankel-norm approximation, with a phase template fitted to the error-magnitude derivative at dominant peaks. We integrate this guidance with dominant-peak selection, focused frequency grids, and adaptive pole-residue and polynomial-coefficient updates to address stagnation and improve approximation accuracy. CD-player examples and benchmarks with known optimal errors evaluate the algorithm against established methods. Comparisons with classical sequential convex optimization (SCO) under shared settings for the common components assess the combined contribution of phase guidance and adaptive frequency refinement. Results demonstrate improved accuracy in most of the tested cases and attainment of the known optimum from four tested initializations for a constructed order-42 benchmark, with additional computation in some cases.

</details>

Abstract: Existing millimeter-wave (mmWave) communication beam patterns spatially weight human-scattered signals and shape the received-power signatures induced by human motion. We exploit these beam-shaped signatures for device-free walkingpace and walk-direction classification using a wall-reflected 60.8-GHz link with closely spaced phased arrays and fixed communication beamforming vectors. Human movement alters the interference between wall-reflected and scattered signals, producing power oscillations surrounding a central blockage fade. A beam-dependent interference model provides a physical interpretation of these signatures. Using received-power statistics from a continuous-tone experiment, we extract fade duration for walking-pace classification and pre/post-fade asymmetries for walk-direction classification. Measurements comprise 144 crossings from three participants along perpendicular and diagonal trajectories. Under leave-one-participant-out validation, logistic regression achieves 86.1% binary and 79.2% three-class walking pace accuracy, and 77.1% travel-direction accuracy. The results demonstrate mobility sensing using fixed communication beams without channel estimation, Doppler processing, or dedicated radar hardware

</details>

Abstract: Registering a segmented CT to fluoroscopy can locate every fracture fragment but can guide surgery only when trusted. After each shot, our closed loop registers all fragments, bounds their errors with a calibrated sandwich covariance, flags implausible or ambiguous ones, and stops or plans the next view. On simulated fluoroscopy of 60 held-out fracture CTs with realistic image uncertainty, 95% bounds covered 83% of unflagged fragments and 91% of those within 5mm, but from the second view on 14% failed by over 5mm, a third with bounds of at most 2mm. A fragment's shift when a view was added separated tightly bounded failures best (exploratory; AUC 0.94, bound 0.81). Requiring that the newest view moved no fragment beyond its bound, preferred post hoc, lowered false stops at 2mm from 23% to 6% overall, with 345 instead of 362 correct ones but more trajectories uncertified; delaying the stop did not, and other signals at equal acceptance left 10-15% false. With a segmentation error shared by all views (median Dice 0.89), 21% of this rule's stops were false against 10% without it (15 cases; exploratory). Minimax planning certified sooner than the clinical protocol without the check (4.20 versus 4.72 shots, Holm p <0.001), but no criterion reached the pre-specified endpoint, a true 2mm error, significantly sooner, even in the true basin. On real fluoroscopy of five intact pelves no 2mm certificate was false. Trust takes evidence across views, not only a calibrated local bound.

</details>

Abstract: Existing generative model-based approaches to linear inverse problems typically use the acquired noisy measurement to condition or guide sampling in the image domain. In this work, we introduce Dual Flow, a novel joint flow model for solving linear inverse problems by jointly estimating the clean image and its corresponding noise-free measurement from the observation. Specifically, Dual Flow formulates image reconstruction and measurement denoising as a coupled estimation problem, using coupled flows in the image and measurement domains. The acquired noisy measurement $\mathbf{y}_0$ is used only to initialize the measurement state; thereafter, the image and measurement states are jointly updated based on their current values. By construction, the final image and measurement estimates are guaranteed to satisfy the underlying forward model. We derive the sampling updates for the joint flow and show that they are governed by the conditional mean of the clean image given the current joint state. We further derive a closed-form expression for a sufficient statistic of the joint state and show that the required conditional mean can be obtained by solving an image-domain Gaussian denoising problem involving operator-dependent, correlated noise. We train a velocity-prediction network using this formulation to estimate the conditional mean from the derived sufficient statistic. Dual Flow requires no hyperparameter tuning beyond selecting the number of inference steps and achieves substantially shorter reconstruction times than competing methods. Extensive experiments across various linear inverse problems and image datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in both distortion metrics and perceptual quality. Additional analyses assess robustness to model mismatch, as well as the ability to generate diverse plausible reconstructions and characterize reconstruction uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract: Depth-resolved attenuation in optical coherence tomography (OCT) can provide quantitative optical contrast beyond structural imaging. However, conventional reconstruction methods can be biased when the OCT signal does not fully decay within the imaging range or when the distal signal tail is dominated by noise or residual reflections from distal sample or support interfaces. Here, we present a perturbation-based Beer-Lambert framework that combines a zeroth-order exponential estimate with a first-order correction, reducing reliance on the unreliable distal signal tail below the current depth. Numerical A-line simulations, Monte Carlo modeling, multilayer silicone-dye-TiO2 phantoms, and ex vivo bovine retina experiments were used to evaluate the method under finite-depth and heterogeneous imaging conditions. The numerical A-line tests showed reduced depth-dependent error against prescribed attenuation profiles; the Monte Carlo comparisons showed less distal drift relative to model-defined benchmarks, while the phantom profiles were compared with separately acquired single-layer OCT calibration values. In a Monte Carlo component test that withheld vessel-mask and vessel-boundary information from reconstruction and used that information only for post hoc evaluation, the first-order correction recovered 61% of the segmentation-informed target vessel contrast and reduced vessel ROI mean absolute error by 65% relative to the fixed zeroth-order baseline. These results suggest that perturbation-based reconstruction may provide a practical approach for depth-resolved reconstruction of effective OCT attenuation when the fully decayed A-line condition is not satisfied.

</details>


### [362] [TOP: Transmission-Oriented Remote Sensing Pansharpening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06430)

Abstract: Pansharpening has been extensively studied as a fundamental image fusion task in remote sensing. However, most existing methods focus on improving fusion quality under the implicit assumption that high-resolution observations have already been fully downlinked to the ground. As a result, existing models are highly vulnerable to perturbations introduced by lossy compression used for downlink transmission. To address this limitation, we present, to the best of our knowledge, the first transmission oriented pansharpening (TOP) framework, which unifies compact latent coding, onboard pansharpening, and efficient downlink transmission within a single satellite-toground pipeline. TOP encodes remote-sensing observations into a compact binary latent space, where a lightweight onboard fusion module can efficiently predict the fused binary latent code. Since only the compact fused code is downlinked, the transmission payload is substantially reduced. Overall, TOP enables low-payload downlink and lightweight onboard processing while maintaining high fusion quality under severe bandwidth constraints. Experiments across multispectral and hyperspectral settings show that TOP consistently achieves superior fusion quality at higher compression ratios than representative pansharpening methods paired with standard lossy codecs. For example, on GaoFen-2, TOP attains a fusion PSNR of 31.4 dB at a downlink compression ratio of 50x. This design also points toward transmissionoriented processing for other remote-sensing tasks. The code will be made available at https://github.com/JingZe-Liang/TOP.git.

</details>


### [363] [From Vision to Context: A Review of Prompt-Driven Target Volume Segmentation in Radiotherapy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06570)

Abstract: Accurate target volume (TV) segmentation is essential for radiotherapy (RT) planning but remains time-consuming. Moreover, the final contour depends on the expertise of the radiation oncologist. Although deep learning has improved medical image segmentation, image-only models cannot encode the clinical reasoning that governs TV delineation, particularly for the clinical target volume (CTV), where boundaries are defined by clinical knowledge rather than imaging features alone. Prompt-driven segmentation has recently emerged as a strategy to address this limitation. This work aims to review these approaches for RT TV segmentation with four objectives: (1) compare the different prompting techniques used; (2) compare their performance against image-only baselines; (3) assess how they are evaluated against standard metrics, including the Dice similarity coefficient (DSC), 95th Hausdorff distance, sensitivity, precision, and clinical assessment; and (4) identify the gaps needed for clinical translation. We systematically searched PubMed, Embase, and arXiv for studies incorporating geometric, clinical text, or interactive prompts into deep learning models for RT TV delineation. Thirteen studies met the inclusion criteria, spanning head and neck, breast, prostate, lung, cervix, and rectal cancers. Seven studies used clinical text prompting, four used geometric prompting, and two used interactive prompting; three combined modalities. Reported DSC ranged from 0.67 to 0.93, compared to 0.65 to 0.91 for image-only baselines. None reported sensitivity or precision. Clinical text prompting appears well suited to CTV delineation. Evidence remains limited, and standardized prompt engineering and clinical validation are needed before these approaches can be translated into routine RT practice.

</details>
